- Iteration is a normal part of analytical work.
- Reproducible records distinguish disciplined experimentation from random trial and error.
38. Q: Why does exposure to different datasets help develop lateral thinking?
A: Show answer
Different datasets reveal different kinds of problems, structures, measurement choices, and analytical opportunities. A learner who works only with one familiar spreadsheet may become accustomed to its assumptions and formats. Exposure to text, images, time series, geographic records, survey responses, or operational transactions encourages the learner to adapt methods and ask new questions. The chapter describes this variety as a way to strengthen lateral thinking. Variety should still be purposeful: each exercise should teach a concept or develop a capability, rather than simply collect unrelated projects. Comparing several datasets can also help learners recognize which analytical principles transfer and which depend on the domain.
Study instruction:
- "Select three datasets with different formats and write down one method that transfers across all three and one method that must be adapted."
Key point:
- Varied practice builds flexibility across data types and problems.
- Transferable principles become clearer when learners compare different contexts.
39. Q: What is business intelligence, and what activities does the chapter associate with it?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes business intelligence as work that begins with a business question, identifies relevant data, visualizes the information, and presents results to stakeholders. Its primary focus is often descriptive: explaining what has happened through reports, dashboards, and summaries. BI is valuable because organizations need reliable information about performance and operations. The chapter’s concern is not that BI is useless, but that routine reporting can cause analysts to focus on updating outputs while giving too little attention to the underlying data and the questions that could be investigated further. Strong BI depends on accurate data and a clear purpose, just as more advanced analytics does.
Study instruction:
- "Map a recurring business report to its question, data sources, transformations, visualizations, and intended audience."
Key point:
- BI organizes and communicates information about business activity.
- A report is only as reliable as the data and definitions behind it.
40. Q: How does the chapter distinguish business intelligence from data science?
A: Show answer
The chapter characterizes BI as primarily descriptive, answering what happened, while data science extends the investigation toward why it happened, what may happen next, and what action should be taken. Data science can include diagnostic, predictive, and prescriptive analysis, using methods that go beyond routine reporting. The distinction is about analytical purpose rather than a rigid division between job titles or software. BI tools can support sophisticated analysis, and data scientists often create dashboards. The important question is whether the work merely reports established measures or investigates causes, forecasts outcomes, and evaluates possible decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Classify three analytics tasks as descriptive, diagnostic, predictive, or prescriptive and explain what additional evidence each requires."
Key point:
- BI often emphasizes descriptive reporting, while data science can extend to deeper investigation and decision support.
- The distinction depends on the analytical question, not only the software used.
41. Q: What are the four questions in the chapter’s analytic value escalator?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents four progressively demanding questions: what happened, why did it happen, what will happen, and what should we do. “What happened” is descriptive analytics, which summarizes historical observations. “Why did it happen” is diagnostic analysis, which investigates drivers and possible explanations. “What will happen” is predictive analysis, which estimates future outcomes under stated assumptions. “What should we do” is prescriptive analysis, which compares possible actions and their consequences. The levels build on one another but are not automatic steps: a decision may require a simple descriptive report, while a predictive model may be inappropriate if the data is weak or the action cannot be controlled.
Study instruction:
- "Take one operational issue and write a distinct analytical question for each of the four levels, noting the extra assumptions needed at each stage."
Key point:
- The four levels progress from description to diagnosis, prediction, and action.
- More advanced analysis is useful only when its assumptions and decision context are sound.
42. Q: What is descriptive analytics, and when is it sufficient?
A: Show answer
Descriptive analytics summarizes observed data to show what has happened. Examples include monthly expenditure reports, sales by region, customer counts, or the number of service requests completed. It is sufficient when the decision-maker needs a reliable status update or a comparison against a target and does not need an explanation or forecast. It can also be the first step in a wider investigation. The chapter warns that organizations can become overly focused on producing recurring reports, which may limit their ability to question the underlying patterns. A descriptive result should therefore be clear about its period, definitions, exclusions, and data quality.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a recurring report and identify the decision it supports, the time period, the key definitions, and the limitations of a descriptive summary."
Key point:
- Descriptive analytics explains what the data records show.
- A clear status report is valuable, but it does not automatically explain causes or future outcomes.
43. Q: What is diagnostic analytics, and how does it differ from simply reporting a trend?
A: Show answer
Diagnostic analytics investigates possible reasons for an observed result. If expenditure increases, a descriptive report shows the increase; a diagnostic analysis examines whether it relates to transaction volume, price changes, project activity, timing, coding, or a one-off event. The analyst may compare groups, inspect records, interview process owners, or test alternative explanations. A correlation or pattern does not by itself establish causation, so conclusions should distinguish evidence from hypotheses. The chapter positions diagnostic analysis as a step beyond reporting because it asks why a result occurred and seeks to identify factors that can inform a response.
Study instruction:
- "Select an unexpected trend and prepare a diagnostic plan with possible explanations, evidence sources, and checks against confounding factors."
Key point:
- Diagnostic analytics investigates possible drivers of an observed outcome.
- A plausible explanation must be tested before it is presented as a cause.
44. Q: What is predictive analytics, and what limitations should decision-makers understand?
A: Show answer
Predictive analytics uses historical and current information to estimate what may happen under specified conditions. Examples include forecasting demand, predicting equipment failure, or estimating the likelihood of a customer leaving a service. A prediction is not a certainty and depends on the quality and relevance of the data, the stability of the relationships, and the assumptions built into the model. Changes in policy, behaviour, technology, or economic conditions can make historical patterns less reliable. Decision-makers should ask how the model was validated, what uncertainty surrounds its outputs, and whether performance is monitored over time. Predictions should inform judgment rather than be treated as facts about the future.
Study instruction:
- "For one forecast, identify the target, time horizon, training data, validation method, uncertainty range, and conditions that could make the model unreliable."
Key point:
- Predictive analytics estimates possible future outcomes rather than guaranteeing them.
- Models need validation and monitoring when the environment changes.
45. Q: What is prescriptive analytics, and why does it require more than a prediction?
A: Show answer
Prescriptive analytics asks what action should be taken in light of possible outcomes, constraints, and objectives. A forecast may estimate demand, but a prescriptive approach considers how much inventory to order, what service level to target, and how cost, capacity, and risk should be balanced. It may use optimization, simulation, decision rules, or scenario analysis. The chapter’s value escalator places this question at the most action-oriented end of analytics. A recommendation still depends on the objective selected and the constraints included. If important stakeholder values or operational risks are omitted, an apparently optimal solution may be unacceptable in practice.
Study instruction:
- "Convert a forecast into a decision problem by specifying the objective, constraints, alternatives, trade-offs, and approval needed before action."
Key point:
- Prescriptive analytics links evidence to a choice among actions.
- Recommendations are only as sound as their objectives, constraints, and governance.
46. Q: Why can routine reporting weaken an organization’s analytical capability?
A: Show answer
The chapter warns that repeated reporting can shift attention from understanding the data to simply delivering the next updated report. Over time, teams may reproduce the same measures without checking whether definitions remain appropriate, whether the data has changed, or whether new questions have emerged. This creates a risk that reports become procedural outputs rather than tools for learning. A healthier reporting process preserves data quality checks, investigates unusual movements, reviews whether indicators still match decisions, and allows stakeholders to challenge assumptions. Routine reporting should provide a stable foundation for analysis, not prevent analysts from exploring causes and opportunities.
Study instruction:
- "Review one recurring report and identify which steps are automated, which checks are still required, and what new question the report could help investigate."
Key point:
- Reporting can become mechanical if attention shifts away from the underlying data.
- A strong BI process includes ongoing review of definitions, quality, and analytical opportunities.
47. Q: Why does the chapter caution against relying exclusively on Excel as a model of data analysis?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that spreadsheets can make data look simpler and more uniform than it really is. Users may place numbers, text, formulas, links, and other content in the same column, mixing raw information with logic and presentation. This flexibility is convenient for many tasks but can encourage weak structure, inconsistent data types, and hidden dependencies. The chapter does not mean that Excel is useless; it warns that familiarity with spreadsheets should not define the limits of a person’s analytical thinking. More formal analytical environments encourage explicit data structures, reproducible transformations, and separation between the data and the code that operates on it.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect a spreadsheet and identify where raw data, formulas, labels, and presentation elements have been mixed in ways that could cause errors."
Key point:
- Excel is useful but its flexibility can hide structural weaknesses.
- Analysts should understand data types and transformations beyond the spreadsheet interface.
48. Q: What does it mean to separate data from logic in an analytical system?
A: Show answer
Separating data from logic means keeping the records being analysed distinct from the formulas, code, and rules used to transform or interpret them. When data and logic are mixed together, a change to a formula or manual edit can silently alter results or make it difficult to reproduce an analysis. A clearer design stores source records in controlled tables or files and applies documented transformations through scripts, queries, or well-defined formulas. This makes it easier to test, review, version, and rerun the process. The chapter uses database management systems and programming environments to illustrate why explicit structure matters for reliable analysis.
Study instruction:
- "Take a spreadsheet-based process and sketch how source records, transformation rules, calculations, and output reports could be separated."
Key point:
- Separation of data and logic improves reproducibility and control.
- Documented transformations make analytical results easier to review and maintain.
49. Q: Why does the chapter mention R and Python as alternatives to Excel?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies R and Python as useful environments for analysing datasets because they support structured data handling, programming, statistical methods, visualization, and reproducible workflows. Unlike an ad hoc spreadsheet, a script can record the steps applied to data and be rerun when new records arrive. The choice between R, Python, Excel, or another tool should depend on the task, the team’s skills, the data environment, and maintenance needs. A programming language is not automatically more accurate than a spreadsheet; poorly written code can also produce errors. The benefit comes from using tools that make the analytical process explicit and testable.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one repeated analysis and compare how it could be performed in Excel and in a script, including reproducibility, maintenance, and review requirements."
Key point:
- R and Python support programmable and reproducible analysis.
- Tool choice should reflect the task and control needs, not a belief that one tool is always superior.
50. Q: Why should data scientists keep ethics in mind from the beginning of a project?
A: Show answer
Data projects can affect privacy, reputation, access to services, security, and personal freedom. The chapter uses examples involving phone records, CCTV, cookies, machine access to information, and the possibility of harmful uses of digital blueprints to show that data can help people but can also be misused. Ethical questions should therefore shape the project before collection and deployment, not be added only after a problem appears. Practitioners should ask who is affected, what purpose justifies collection, who can access the information, what decisions may be made, and how errors or harms can be challenged. Ethics is part of project quality and public trust.
Study instruction:
- "Before starting a data project, prepare an impact checklist covering affected people, purpose, data access, potential harms, accountability, and redress."
Key point:
- Ethical risks arise throughout the data lifecycle, not only at the point of analysis.
- Early assessment makes it easier to design proportionate safeguards.
51. Q: How does the chapter’s motorcycle number-plate story illustrate the positive use of personal data?
A: Show answer
The chapter recounts a situation in which police contacted the author because a motorcycle bearing his number plate had been involved in an incident. The author’s own vehicle was still at home, and he reflected that location records from his phone could help establish where he had been. The example illustrates how digital traces may provide evidence that supports an investigation or protects an innocent person. It does not mean that location data is infallible or that recorded data always tells the complete truth. Device sharing, inaccurate timestamps, spoofing, gaps in coverage, and mistaken identity can all affect interpretation. Evidence should be corroborated and handled through lawful procedures.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the digital records that might corroborate an alibi and explain why each record needs validation and contextual interpretation."
Key point:
- Data can support accountability and help establish facts in an investigation.
- Digital records are evidence to assess, not unquestionable proof on their own.
52. Q: Why is the statement “recorded data doesn’t lie” too simplistic for forensic analysis?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses the phrase to emphasize the value of digital records, but a forensic analyst must recognize that data can be incomplete, altered, misattributed, or misunderstood. A phone may be used by another person, a location signal may be inaccurate, a system clock may be wrong, and a record may lack the context needed to interpret it. Investigators must establish provenance, preserve evidence, document collection methods, check chain of custody, and corroborate important conclusions with independent sources. The data itself may be authentic while the inference drawn from it is incorrect. Forensic work therefore depends on both technical integrity and careful reasoning.
Study instruction:
- "For a hypothetical location record, list checks for device ownership, timestamp accuracy, collection method, possible sharing, and independent corroboration."
Key point:
- Authentic records can still support incorrect interpretations.
- Forensic conclusions require provenance, integrity, context, and corroboration.
53. Q: How can phone records and CCTV help in a forensic investigation, and what safeguards are needed?
A: Show answer
Phone records and CCTV may help reconstruct movement, establish timing, identify events, or corroborate witness accounts. Their value depends on whether the records are authentic, complete, lawfully obtained, and relevant to the question being investigated. Investigators should preserve original data, record how it was collected, restrict access, and avoid exposing unrelated personal information. They should also consider whether the camera clock is synchronized, whether the footage covers the relevant area, and whether a phone was carried by the person under investigation. Because these records can reveal sensitive patterns about innocent people, access and retention must be controlled under applicable law and policy.
Study instruction:
- "Create an evidence-handling checklist for digital records that covers lawful access, preservation, metadata, chain of custody, relevance, and privacy."
Key point:
- Digital traces can corroborate events and timelines.
- Evidence handling must protect integrity and the rights of people captured in the records.
54. Q: What ethical risks arise when machines gain access to very large amounts of information?
A: Show answer
Large-scale access can allow machines to identify patterns and make predictions beyond what an individual person could process, but it can also enable intrusive profiling, manipulation, surveillance, or the misuse of personal and organizational information. The chapter notes that increasing computing power, data access, and algorithmic sophistication can expand both beneficial and harmful capabilities. The risk depends on the system’s purpose, permissions, safeguards, and the consequences of its outputs. Organizations should define access boundaries, test for misuse, log sensitive operations, restrict high-impact actions, and provide oversight. Technical capability should not be treated as a sufficient reason to grant unrestricted access.
Study instruction:
- "For a proposed AI system, define the minimum data access it needs and identify what misuse could occur if its permissions were expanded."
Key point:
- More capable systems can amplify both beneficial analysis and harmful misuse.
- Access should be limited to what the task justifies and monitored throughout use.
55. Q: Why does the chapter use 3D printing to illustrate the ethical consequences of data access?
A: Show answer
The chapter observes that a digital blueprint can be used to produce a physical object through 3D printing. As the technology becomes cheaper and more accessible, the same capability can support useful manufacturing or enable the creation of dangerous items. The example shows that data is not always passive information: a file can act as an instruction for a machine that changes the physical world. Ethical and regulatory questions therefore extend beyond privacy to include safety, misuse, ownership, and control over digital designs. A responsible approach considers the potential consequences of a dataset or file being used by an automated production system.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a digital design file and assess its intended use, possible harmful use, access permissions, and safeguards before allowing production."
Key point:
- Digital information can enable physical actions when connected to production technology.
- Risk assessment should consider downstream uses, not only the file’s immediate content.
56. Q: How can unrestricted access to online data support manipulation or propaganda?
A: Show answer
The chapter warns that systems with access to large amounts of online information may be used to analyse behaviour, impersonate people, or distribute persuasive messages at scale. Such uses can exploit personal interests, social connections, and patterns of attention. The risk is not that every AI system will manipulate users, but that powerful tools can make targeted influence easier when access and purpose are poorly controlled. Safeguards include provenance checks, transparency about automated content, limits on sensitive profiling, platform monitoring, and public education about manipulated information. Analysts should also distinguish evidence of coordinated activity from assumptions based only on unusual patterns.
Study instruction:
- "Map a hypothetical data-driven influence campaign and identify the data sources, targeting mechanism, possible harms, and controls that could reduce misuse."
Key point:
- Large-scale data can support highly targeted influence and impersonation.
- Transparency, access controls, and evidence-based monitoring help limit abuse.
57. Q: What are internet cookies, and why does the chapter discuss them as an ethical issue?
A: Show answer
Cookies are small data files stored by a website or service on a user’s device and later read to support functions such as remembering preferences, maintaining sessions, or tracking activity. The chapter discusses them because cookies can record pages visited and interactions, including information used for personalization or advertising. The ethical issue is not that every cookie is harmful, but that users may not understand what is collected, who receives it, how long it is retained, or how it is combined with other data. Consent and transparency requirements vary by jurisdiction and type of cookie, so organizations must follow current applicable rules rather than rely on historical examples in the chapter.
Study instruction:
- "Classify cookie uses as essential service functions, preference storage, analytics, or advertising and identify the notice or consent requirements that may apply."
Key point:
- Cookies can support useful functions and also enable tracking.
- Requirements depend on purpose and jurisdiction, so current legal guidance must be checked.
58. Q: Why should a data scientist not assume that public acceptance of tracking means informed consent?
A: Show answer
People may become accustomed to data collection because it is embedded in everyday services, but familiarity does not prove that they understand or freely accept each use. A person may click through a notice without understanding the data categories, recipients, retention, or consequences. The chapter reflects on how social attitudes toward cookies changed as digital services became normal, but a shift in habit should not be treated as a waiver of rights. Responsible collection requires clear explanations, meaningful choices where required, and respect for legal obligations. Consent should also be distinguished from other lawful grounds for processing where applicable; it should never be assumed from silence alone.
Study instruction:
- "Review a privacy notice and assess whether an ordinary user could understand the purpose, data categories, recipients, retention, and available choices."
Key point:
- Normalization of tracking does not equal informed agreement.
- Transparency and valid legal grounds must be assessed for each data use.
59. Q: What is data governance, and why is it necessary as technology develops?
A: Show answer
Data governance is the framework of roles, rules, decision rights, and controls that determines how information is collected, described, accessed, shared, protected, retained, and used. The chapter points to public debate and institutional work on data management and governance as a response to the speed of technological change. Governance helps organizations define who is accountable, what uses are permitted, how quality is maintained, and how harms or disputes are handled. It is not only a technical policy document: it requires leadership, staff understanding, monitoring, and enforcement. Without governance, the organization may gain analytical capability while losing control over privacy, security, and accountability.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a basic governance map for one dataset, assigning an owner, steward, approved users, retention rule, and escalation route."
Key point:
- Governance turns ethical intentions into defined responsibilities and controls.
- Technology should develop alongside rules for access, quality, security, and accountability.
60. Q: Why does the chapter argue that ethical frameworks should evolve alongside technology?
A: Show answer
New technologies can create uses and risks faster than legislation, organizational policies, and public understanding can respond. The chapter raises the concern that the rate of technical development may outpace society’s ability to establish suitable safeguards. An ethical framework helps an organization reason about new applications before every scenario is covered by a specific law. It should identify principles, affected stakeholders, risk assessment, approval requirements, and ongoing review. Frameworks do not replace legislation or professional obligations; they help translate those requirements into project decisions and identify issues that may need escalation or policy development.
Study instruction:
- "For an emerging technology, identify the likely gap between capability and current rules, then specify an interim ethical review process."
Key point:
- Governance and ethical review need to adapt as capabilities change.
- An internal framework supplements, but does not replace, law and formal accountability.
61. Q: How should a data scientist balance technological innovation with privacy and public protection?
A: Show answer
The chapter rejects the idea that society must either stop technological progress or accept every new data use without question. A more practical balance is to pursue benefits while identifying foreseeable harms, limiting unnecessary collection, protecting access, testing systems, and giving affected people meaningful ways to understand or challenge outcomes. The balance may require delaying a deployment, narrowing its purpose, changing a model, or adding independent oversight. It also requires reassessment as the system and its context change. Responsible innovation is not simply avoiding risk; it is demonstrating that the expected benefit justifies the remaining risk and that safeguards are effective.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a benefit-risk assessment for a proposed data project and identify conditions under which it should be redesigned, delayed, or rejected."
Key point:
- Innovation and protection should be designed together.
- A project should proceed only with proportionate benefits, safeguards, and accountability.
62. Q: What is the difference between using data to improve a process and using data to justify a predetermined decision?
A: Show answer
Using data to improve a process begins with a question and examines evidence that may confirm, challenge, or refine an initial belief. Using data to justify a predetermined decision starts with the desired conclusion and selects measures or interpretations that support it. The latter creates confirmation bias, selective reporting, and a risk of misleading stakeholders. The chapter’s emphasis on creativity and ethics implies that analysts should remain open to unexpected findings while maintaining methodological discipline. They should record the original question, disclose exclusions and assumptions, test alternative explanations, and report results that do not support the preferred outcome.
Study instruction:
- "Review a management report and identify whether the analysis tested a question or merely selected figures that support an existing decision."
Key point:
- Analysis should be capable of challenging the analyst’s initial assumptions.
- Selective evidence undermines trust and can lead to poor decisions.
63. Q: How can a data scientist document assumptions and decisions to make analysis trustworthy?
A: Show answer
A trustworthy analysis records where the data came from, what each field means, how missing or unusual values were handled, which records were excluded, what transformations were applied, and why a particular method was selected. It should also document assumptions, uncertainty, validation results, and known limitations. This record allows another person to reproduce the work, challenge an interpretation, and understand whether the result remains valid when the data changes. Documentation is especially important when analysis informs finance, healthcare, law enforcement, employment, or public services. The chapter’s emphasis on asking people for context and keeping ethics in view reinforces the need to preserve both technical and decision-related reasoning.
Study instruction:
- "Create a one-page analysis log template with fields for source, purpose, definitions, transformations, assumptions, validation, limitations, and approval."
Key point:
- Documentation supports reproducibility, review, and accountability.
- Assumptions and limitations should be visible rather than hidden in code or memory.
64. Q: How can a beginner turn existing skills into a practical data science portfolio?
A: Show answer
The chapter suggests reflecting on personal and professional experience, recording transferable skills, and using them to identify projects where data can solve a real problem. A useful portfolio should demonstrate how the learner framed a question, obtained and assessed data, selected a method, interpreted the result, and communicated its limitations. It should not consist only of attractive charts or lists of software packages. Projects using public data can show analytical ability without exposing confidential information. A portfolio can also include stakeholder communication, visual design, domain knowledge, and ethical judgment, making it easier for employers to understand the learner’s broader contribution.
Study instruction:
- "Plan one portfolio project that uses an area of existing expertise and produces a reproducible analysis, a clear visualization, and a short limitations note."
Key point:
- A portfolio should demonstrate problem-solving from question to communication.
- Existing domain and soft skills can distinguish a beginner’s work.
65. Q: What is the most important integrated lesson of Chapter 3 for someone entering data science?
A: Show answer
The chapter’s integrated lesson is that becoming effective in data science is a continuing process of learning how to ask better questions, understand context, practise with varied data, communicate with people, and use technology responsibly. Beginners do not need to master every algorithm before contributing, but they must avoid confusing easy access to tools with professional competence. Creativity and domain knowledge help identify valuable problems; practice develops technical judgment; business intelligence provides a starting point for deeper analytics; and ethical thinking protects the people affected by data use. The strongest data scientist is not simply the person who can run the most advanced model, but the person who can produce reliable, useful, understandable, and responsible work.
Study instruction:
- "Use the five attributes to assess one project from start to finish and identify the strongest capability and the most important development gap."
Key point:
- Effective data science combines technical practice with context, communication, creativity, and ethics.
- The purpose of analysis is to create trustworthy insight that supports a meaningful decision.
Rows: 65
Table 4
Identifying and Refining Data Science Questions
1. Q: What does the chapter mean by the “identify the question” stage of a data science project?
A: Show answer
It is the disciplined work of turning an initial business issue or request into a clearly understood, answerable problem before data preparation and analysis begin. The analyst establishes what decision or outcome the work is meant to support, why it matters, whose needs are involved, what is known about the operating context, and what information could answer it. The chapter treats this as the first stage of the Data Science Process, not as administrative overhead. Its central warning is that collecting or modelling large volumes of data cannot compensate for investigating the wrong question.
Study instruction:
- Explain the purpose of problem identification and distinguish it from data preparation, analysis, and reporting.
Key point:
- Define the problem before selecting data or methods.
- Analytical effort is useful only when it addresses the intended need.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Government guidance on robust analysis
2. Q: Why should a data scientist resist the pressure to begin by preparing data immediately?
A: Show answer
Starting with available tables, dashboards, or algorithms can create activity without establishing whether that activity is relevant. A dataset may be large and technically accessible yet contain no variables capable of answering the real business concern. Premature preparation also encourages analysts to let the available fields dictate the question, rather than selecting evidence in light of the decision that needs to be made. The chapter notes that problem identification may use little or no data, which can make it feel unproductive, but this early reasoning reduces wasted cleaning, analysis, and rework. The appropriate response to pressure is to explain what must be clarified and why that work protects the project’s outcome.
Study instruction:
- Take a request to “analyse the database” and list the decisions that must be clarified before extracting or cleaning records.
Key point:
- Data availability is not a substitute for a defined analytical purpose.
- Early scoping can prevent technically sound work on the wrong problem.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Engagement and scoping
3. Q: What is the difference between a business issue, an analytical question, and a proposed task?
A: Show answer
A business issue describes an unsatisfactory condition, such as falling sales, customer attrition, or a recurring product defect. An analytical question states what must be learned about that condition, for example which customer segments have the highest observed churn and how churn varies over time. A proposed task is an activity someone suggests, such as building a dashboard, running a regression, or extracting transaction data. The task is not automatically the correct response to the issue. The analyst should first establish the underlying decision need, then determine whether the proposed task can produce evidence relevant to it. Several different analytical questions or methods may be needed to address one issue.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite three vague business issues as answerable questions, then identify one possible task for each and explain why the task is not the question itself.
Key point:
- Issues describe conditions; questions specify what must be learned.
- Tasks are methods or deliverables and must be justified by the question.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Engagement and scoping
4. Q: How can a vague request such as “we have lots of data, find us some insights” be made actionable?
A: Show answer
Ask the requester what is not working, what decision is pending, what would change if the analysis succeeded, and which people or processes are affected. Clarify the population, unit of analysis, relevant period, business outcome, constraints, and the form of evidence needed. Replace the open-ended request with a bounded question, such as identifying which customer groups have declining repeat purchases during a specified period and which operational factors are associated with that pattern. Check whether the required fields and records exist before promising an answer. A useful question is specific enough to guide data selection but not so narrow that it assumes the conclusion in advance.
Study instruction:
- Draft a one-page question brief for a vague request, including decision, population, outcome, time period, stakeholders, and data feasibility.
Key point:
- Convert an open-ended request into a decision-relevant, testable question.
- Confirm that the data could plausibly answer it before committing to a method.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Engagement and scoping
5. Q: Why must an analyst check whether the question supplied by a colleague is actually the right problem to investigate?
A: Show answer
A colleague may understand the operational symptom but not the data requirements, causal structure, or decision context. Their suggested questions may encode an untested assumption, omit an affected department, request information that is not recorded, or focus on a proxy that does not represent the real outcome. Blindly accepting them can produce no result or a polished answer to the wrong question. The chapter assigns the analyst responsibility for deconstructing the issue rather than simply executing another person’s first formulation. This does not mean disregarding the requester; it means testing the framing collaboratively and documenting any changes so that the eventual analysis remains connected to the original need.
Study instruction:
- Review a stakeholder’s proposed question and mark its assumptions, missing definitions, data dependencies, and decision implications.
Key point:
- Treat a proposed question as a starting hypothesis about the problem, not as an unquestionable specification.
- Refine it with the people who understand the operational context.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Engagement and scoping
6. Q: What should an analyst learn from the person who originally raised the problem?
A: Show answer
The initial discussion should establish what the problem is, why it needs attention now, what has already been tried, who is affected, who owns the relevant process, and what the organisation would regard as a useful resolution. Ask what triggered the request, whether there is a deadline or external commitment, what consequences follow if the issue remains unresolved, and which decisions the analysis can realistically influence. Also ask how the requester currently interprets the issue and what evidence supports that interpretation. These questions reveal assumptions and dependencies that may not appear in a ticket or engagement letter. The answers should be recorded so that the analyst and sponsor can compare their understanding before work expands.
Study instruction:
- Prepare and rehearse a stakeholder interview guide that covers urgency, impact, history, decision rights, affected groups, and success criteria.
Key point:
- The requester’s context is essential input to problem definition.
- Record the rationale and expected use of the analysis, not just the requested output.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Engagement and scoping
7. Q: Why is it important to understand why a problem must be solved now?
A: Show answer
Timing helps distinguish an urgent operational decision from a general desire to explore data. The reason may be a product launch, a regulatory or contractual deadline, an emerging service failure, a budget decision, or a time-limited opportunity. Urgency affects the feasible scope, the acceptable level of precision, the data period, and the people who must be consulted. It may also reveal that the proposed analysis cannot be completed before the decision date, requiring an interim answer or a revised expectation. The analyst should not allow urgency to erase basic validity checks; instead, the reason for the deadline should inform a proportionate design and explicit statement of limitations.
Study instruction:
- For a sample project, write down the decision date, the event driving it, the latest useful delivery date, and the checks that cannot safely be omitted.
Key point:
- The reason for urgency shapes scope and feasibility.
- A rushed answer must still disclose material uncertainty and limitations.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Proportionality and engagement
8. Q: How should unexpected findings or useful detours be handled during problem identification?
A: Show answer
The chapter encourages analysts to notice unexpected information because exploration may reveal additional business value. However, an interesting detour should not silently replace the agreed problem. Record the observation, its evidence, possible significance, and whether it requires a separate question or follow-up project. Then assess it with the sponsor against the original objective, available resources, and decision timetable. If it is material to the current question, revise the scope with agreement; if not, preserve it in an opportunity or issue log for later consideration. This approach combines curiosity with discipline and prevents exploratory work from turning into uncontrolled scope expansion.
Study instruction:
- Create a simple detour register with fields for observation, evidence, relevance, owner, decision, and whether separate approval is required.
Key point:
- Be open to discovery without losing control of the agreed question.
- Separate valuable new work from the current project unless scope is formally revised.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Analytical lifecycle and assurance
9. Q: How does industry knowledge improve the framing of a data science problem?
A: Show answer
Industry knowledge helps the analyst recognise common operating models, terminology, constraints, market conditions, and plausible causes of the issue. It can suggest which departments hold relevant information, which measures are meaningful, and which apparent relationships may have an operational explanation. For example, a low-margin product may be retained because it supports sales of a more profitable product or is being phased out; a database-only interpretation could therefore lead to a poor recommendation. The chapter advises researching the sector and comparing the organisation with relevant competitors, while also checking whether those comparisons genuinely apply. Industry knowledge guides investigation but should not be treated as proof that a particular explanation is true.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a short industry-context note that separates verified sector facts, organisation-specific information, and hypotheses requiring data or stakeholder confirmation.
Key point:
- Context makes analytical questions more relevant and interpretable.
- Industry experience generates hypotheses; evidence is still needed to test them.
10. Q: Why should analysts speak with process owners and colleagues rather than relying only on databases?
A: Show answer
Process owners can explain how records are created, what fields mean in practice, which exceptions occur, and where important events are documented outside the main system. Colleagues may identify informal workflows, system changes, operational constraints, or data custodians who were not obvious from the initial request. This is particularly important when a dataset represents only one part of a process or when data definitions differ between departments. The chapter describes the analyst as someone who connects functions that may rarely work together. Conversations should be purposeful and documented; informal explanations need to be checked against records and, where material, confirmed by the responsible owner.
Study instruction:
- Map the business process behind a proposed dataset and identify the people who create, approve, maintain, and use each important record.
Key point:
- Data is produced by organisational processes and people.
- Process knowledge helps reveal missing context and data limitations.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO: Assessing Data Reliability
11. Q: In what sense can a data scientist act as a bridge between departments?
A: Show answer
A data scientist may need information from IT, operations, finance, marketing, customer service, and other functions even when those teams have different priorities or rarely exchange information. The analyst translates the business concern into data requirements, explains technical constraints in operational language, and brings process knowledge back into the analytical design. The chapter’s customer-experience example shows work spanning marketing and IT because database access and business interpretation sat in different places. This bridging role requires maintaining relationships and communicating progress, not merely requesting extracts. It also creates a responsibility to respect each department’s authority, confidentiality obligations, and ownership of its records.
Study instruction:
- Draw a stakeholder and data-flow map for a cross-functional project, showing what each department contributes and what it needs from the analysis.
Key point:
- Cross-functional coordination improves the context and feasibility of analysis.
- The analyst connects expertise without assuming ownership of every process.
Source links:
- Government Analysis Function: Stakeholder mapping
12. Q: What does it mean to “think like a consultant” when identifying a data science question?
A: Show answer
In this chapter, it means stepping back from individual records to examine the organisation, its strategy, its stakeholders, and the wider structure of the problem. The analyst asks how the issue fits into business objectives, which parts of the organisation are affected, who can make decisions, and what success would look like. This top-level view helps narrow a broad issue into questions that matter to the organisation. It does not mean replacing evidence with assumptions or adopting a consultant’s conclusion without testing it. The chapter recommends using strategic context to guide the investigation, then checking that framing against operational information and available data.
Study instruction:
- Write a one-paragraph strategic framing for a data request before listing datasets, fields, or analytical techniques.
Key point:
- Begin with the organisation’s decision context, not with a favourite tool.
- Strategic framing must be tested against operational evidence.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Engagement and scoping
13. Q: What is the difference between a top-down and a bottom-up approach to understanding a business problem?
A: Show answer
A top-down approach begins with organisational goals, executive perspectives, strategy, and high-level reports, then works toward operational detail. A bottom-up approach begins with records, transactions, process data, and frontline activity, then builds an account of what is happening across the organisation. The chapter associates top-down work with consulting and bottom-up work with data science, but these are broad descriptions rather than universal rules about professions. Each approach sees different evidence: top-down framing can reveal strategic intent, while bottom-up analysis can reveal patterns and operational variation. Neither should be treated as a complete picture on its own.
Study instruction:
- Apply both approaches to the same business issue and compare the questions, evidence, and stakeholders each approach reveals.
Key point:
- Top-down starts with organisational context; bottom-up starts with operational evidence.
- Their value lies in the different perspectives they provide.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Analytical design and lifecycle
14. Q: Why can a bottom-up analysis produce a misleading recommendation if it is used alone?
A: Show answer
Records can show that an activity appears unprofitable, inefficient, or unusual without explaining why the organisation performs it. A product with a low direct margin may support sales elsewhere, meet a contractual obligation, serve a strategic customer, or be in a planned phase-out. Similarly, an operational metric may reflect a deliberate policy or a data-capture change rather than poor performance. The chapter warns that numerical findings do not reveal the whole business context. Before recommending action, test the interpretation with process owners, examine dependencies, and determine whether the apparent problem is connected to a broader objective or constraint.
Study instruction:
- Find an example of a negative metric and list at least three plausible operational explanations that should be checked before recommending action.
Key point:
- A pattern in the data is not, by itself, a complete explanation.
- Validate recommendations against strategy and operational context.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO: Assessing Data Reliability
15. Q: Why can a top-down analysis also be misleading if it is used alone?
A: Show answer
High-level reports and executive accounts may be aggregated, delayed, filtered, or based on assumptions that do not reflect how work is performed. They may omit local variation, frontline constraints, unusual transactions, or differences between recorded policy and actual practice. A strategy statement can identify what the organisation wants to achieve, but it cannot establish whether the underlying operational data supports a proposed explanation. The chapter also notes that top-down investigation can take substantial time and may produce a framing that does not match the data. Analysts should therefore test strategic assumptions against operational records and the people who create them.
Study instruction:
- Compare an executive summary with underlying operational records and note where aggregation, timing, or omitted context changes the interpretation.
Key point:
- Strategic context is necessary but not sufficient evidence.
- Check high-level explanations against operational reality.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO: Assessing Data Reliability
16. Q: Why does the chapter recommend combining top-down and bottom-up investigation?
A: Show answer
Combining them links organisational goals and stakeholder knowledge with the detail contained in operational records. The top-down view helps determine which outcomes matter and why; the bottom-up view tests what the data actually records and how processes behave. Differences between reports and source records can then become questions to investigate rather than reasons to trust one side automatically. The combined approach can improve both analytical validity and the likelihood that stakeholders will understand and use the result. It is a practical recommendation from the author, not a guarantee that every project needs identical effort in both directions; the balance should reflect the problem and available resources.
Study instruction:
- Build a two-column evidence plan showing strategic questions and operational tests, then identify where the two views should be reconciled.
Key point:
- Connect strategic intent with operational evidence.
- Use discrepancies between the two views as issues to investigate.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Analytical lifecycle
17. Q: How can stakeholder mapping improve the way a data science project is framed?
A: Show answer
Stakeholder mapping makes the affected and influential groups visible before the analyst settles the question or engagement plan. A practical map can record each stakeholder’s interest, influence, knowledge, decision authority, data responsibility, and the type of engagement required. The Government Analysis Function describes mapping stakeholders by power and interest to help determine who should be managed closely, consulted, kept satisfied, or kept informed. This is a useful planning aid, not a substitute for listening to people with less formal power who may be directly affected. Update the map when the project scope or evidence changes.
Study instruction:
- Create a stakeholder matrix for a real project and justify the engagement approach for each group, including affected users and data custodians.
Key point:
- Identify who is affected, who decides, and who holds relevant knowledge.
- Engagement should be planned and revised as the project develops.
Source links:
- Government Analysis Function: Stakeholder mapping
18. Q: How should an analyst identify the people who have authority to approve or act on the analysis?
A: Show answer
Distinguish the person who requests the work from the person who owns the business process, the person who controls the data, the technical analyst, the reviewer, and the final decision-maker. One individual may hold several roles, but the responsibilities should still be explicit. Ask who can approve the problem statement, provide access, resolve conflicts between departments, accept the deliverable, and authorise changes in scope. If the analysis requires a decision from a committee or senior manager, identify that route early. Clear decision rights reduce the risk of producing a technically complete report that nobody is authorised or prepared to use.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a responsibility table that names the requester, sponsor, process owner, data owner, analyst, reviewer, and decision authority for a sample project.
Key point:
- Requesting, providing, analysing, reviewing, and deciding are distinct responsibilities.
- Identify acceptance and decision authority before analysis begins.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Roles and responsibilities
19. Q: What should “understand the limitations” mean before a project commits to a dataset?
A: Show answer
The analyst should establish at a high level what data exists, where it is held, who controls it, how it was created, what period it covers, and whether access is feasible. Check whether the required variables, population, level of detail, and time coverage are available, and whether records can be linked appropriately. Ask data owners about known gaps, system changes, missing periods, definitions, and restrictions. The chapter stresses that the analyst should not wait until extensive preparation has begun to discover that essential information is absent. This is an early feasibility assessment, not a full technical audit of every field; detailed validation follows when the data is prepared.
Study instruction:
- Complete a data-availability checklist for each proposed source, including owner, location, coverage, fields, access, known gaps, and constraints.
Key point:
- Test data feasibility before promising an analytical result.
- Early checks should identify material gaps without pretending to complete later data validation.
Source links:
- The Government Data Quality Framework
20. Q: What does it mean for data to be fit for the purpose of a particular question?
A: Show answer
Fitness for purpose means that the data is sufficiently suitable for the intended use, not that it is universally perfect. A dataset that is adequate for a broad trend may be inadequate for individual-level decisions, subgroup comparisons, or a precise estimate. Suitability depends on the question, required accuracy, coverage, timeliness, definitions, and consequences of error. The Government Data Quality Framework explicitly treats quality in relation to intended purpose and recognises that the required level can vary by use. The analyst should state which quality dimensions matter for the question and what limitations could change the interpretation or decision.
Study instruction:
- Take one dataset and assess whether it is suitable for two different questions, explaining why the quality requirements differ.
Key point:
- Data quality is purpose-dependent, not an abstract pass-or-fail label.
- Match the quality assessment to the decision and risk.
Source links:
- The Government Data Quality Framework
21. Q: Which data reliability dimensions should be considered when assessing whether a source can support an analysis?
A: Show answer
At minimum, examine accuracy, completeness, and applicability to the intended purpose. Accuracy concerns whether recorded values represent what they claim to represent; completeness concerns whether required records and fields are present; applicability concerns whether the data is suitable for the specific question. Depending on the project, timeliness, validity, consistency, uniqueness, coverage, and comparability may also matter. The U.S. Government Accountability Office uses accuracy, completeness, and applicability when describing data reliability for audit purposes and advocates a risk-based assessment. These dimensions are not interchangeable: a complete dataset can still contain inaccurate values, and accurate records can still be irrelevant to the question.
Study instruction:
- For a sample dataset, define a test and an evidence source for accuracy, completeness, applicability, timeliness, and consistency.
Key point:
- Assess several dimensions rather than relying on a single quality score.
- Explain how each weakness could affect the proposed conclusion.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO: Assessing Data Reliability
- The Government Data Quality Framework
22. Q: Why should data be understood in the context of its lifecycle rather than examined in isolation?
A: Show answer
A field may be entered incorrectly at collection, transformed incorrectly during processing, duplicated during integration, or become stale as systems and definitions change. A snapshot of the final table may not reveal where the problem originated. Understanding the lifecycle means tracing how information is created, recorded, transferred, transformed, stored, maintained, and used. The Government Data Quality Hub warns that data quality assessment can be misleading when the data’s history and processes are ignored. For project framing, this means asking how the proposed source was produced and what controls or transformations have already affected it before treating its values as evidence.
Study instruction:
- Draw the lifecycle of one important data element from initial capture to analysis and mark where errors, omissions, or definition changes could occur.
Key point:
- Data reliability depends on the processes that create and change the data.
- Trace important transformations before interpreting the final dataset.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Hub: Data quality assessment pitfalls
23. Q: How should an analyst assess whether the available data can support a representative or statistically relevant investigation?
A: Show answer
Start by defining the target population, the unit being observed, the period of interest, and the decision or inference the analysis is expected to support. Then compare those requirements with the available coverage, selection process, missing groups, sample size, and likely sources of bias. Ask data owners how records were generated and whether the observed cases differ systematically from cases that are absent. The chapter recommends checking data availability and whether further collection is needed before proceeding. It does not provide a universal sample-size formula; the appropriate design depends on the question, method, variability, precision required, and consequences of error.
Study instruction:
- Write a sampling note that defines the population, unit, coverage, exclusions, selection process, and limits on generalising results.
Key point:
- A large dataset is not necessarily representative.
- Sampling suitability depends on the inference the project intends to make.
Source links:
- U.S. GAO: Assessing Data Reliability
24. Q: What should a project team do when essential data is missing or unavailable?
A: Show answer
First identify exactly which question or decision depends on the missing information and whether another legitimate source can provide it. Discuss the gap with the data owner and process specialists, check whether additional collection is feasible, and assess the time, cost, privacy, and quality implications. Possible responses include narrowing the question, changing the period or population, collecting new information, using a defensible proxy, or reporting that the question cannot currently be answered. A proxy must be justified and its limitations made explicit; it should not be presented as equivalent to the missing measure. Any change that materially affects the expected result should be agreed with the sponsor before analysis continues.
Study instruction:
- For a hypothetical missing variable, compare alternative sources, new collection, a proxy, a revised question, and a no-answer decision.
Key point:
- A data gap is a project decision, not a reason to fabricate certainty.
- Make trade-offs visible and obtain agreement on material changes.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Engagement and scoping
25. Q: What is the purpose of the chapter’s optional early data-mining step?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents early data mining as an exploratory test of the available information, not as the main analysis or a required stage in every project. Simple summaries and visualisations can reveal unusual patterns, possible segments, unexpected gaps, or promising directions for later questions. The author describes this as a way to become familiar with raw data and potentially save effort later. It may produce no useful insight, particularly when data quality is weak or the problem is complex. Any pattern discovered at this stage should be recorded as a lead for investigation, not treated as a confirmed explanation or a final answer to the business question.
Study instruction:
- Design a small exploratory review and label every observation as descriptive, tentative, or requiring further testing.
Key point:
- Early mining can inform question refinement but is optional and exploratory.
- A pattern is a prompt for investigation, not proof of cause.
26. Q: How can analysts avoid confusing exploratory data mining with confirmatory analysis?
A: Show answer
Exploratory work searches for patterns and generates hypotheses; confirmatory analysis tests specified propositions using an appropriate design and stated assumptions. If an analyst inspects many fields and then reports only the strongest association, the apparent result may reflect chance, selective attention, or repeated searching. Record how the pattern was found, which comparisons were made, and what further evidence would be needed. Reframe promising observations as explicit questions, then decide whether a separate sample, additional data, a pre-specified test, or domain review is required. The chapter’s “test drive” metaphor is useful precisely because it signals that early mining is preliminary rather than conclusive.
Study instruction:
- Take an exploratory chart and write a follow-up test plan that specifies the hypothesis, comparison, data needed, and alternative explanations.
Key point:
- Exploration generates leads; validation evaluates them.
- Disclose how a finding was discovered before treating it as evidence for action.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Analytical design and assurance
27. Q: How can a broad business problem be refined into a set of manageable sub-questions?
A: Show answer
Break the issue into components that can be investigated separately, then check how those components relate to the overall outcome. For each sub-question, identify the measure, population, time period, data source, stakeholder, and decision it informs. The chapter illustrates this by moving from a broad concern about profitability to possible revenue and expense drivers, then to more specific categories within each. The decomposition should remain connected to the original problem; a long list of interesting questions is not useful if the answers cannot influence the decision. Keep track of assumptions and allow the structure to change when evidence contradicts the initial framing.
Study instruction:
- Build a problem tree for a real issue and trace each branch to a measurable question and an intended decision.
Key point:
- Decomposition makes broad issues investigable.
- Every sub-question should contribute to the central problem or a documented follow-up.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Engagement and scoping
28. Q: How does the chapter’s low-profitability example demonstrate problem decomposition?
A: Show answer
The initial issue is that company profits are too low. The analyst first considers broad drivers such as revenue and expenses, then subdivides them into more specific questions about expense categories, products, services, and changes over time. Stakeholder knowledge may answer some questions quickly; for example, managers may know whether a major expense category has changed materially. That information can help prioritise the next analysis, but it should not be used to dismiss a possibility without checking the evidence needed for the decision. The example shows how structured questions reduce an unmanageable search across all available data to a focused investigation of plausible contributors.
Study instruction:
- Recreate the profitability problem tree and add the data source and stakeholder needed to test each branch.
Key point:
- Move from an outcome to drivers, then to testable sub-questions.
- Use operational knowledge to focus analysis, while retaining evidence checks.
29. Q: How can an analyst distinguish a root cause from a symptom when refining a question?
A: Show answer
A symptom is an observable condition, such as falling sales, higher complaints, or delayed delivery. A root cause is an underlying factor that contributes to the symptom, such as a supply constraint, a change in customer mix, a process bottleneck, or an unreliable measurement system. The analyst should avoid assuming that the first plausible explanation is causal. Decompose the issue, compare alternative explanations, examine timing and process evidence, and involve people who understand the work. If the available design supports only association or description, report that accurately rather than claiming to have identified a cause. The purpose of refinement is to make the investigation discriminating enough to rule explanations in or out where the evidence allows.
Study instruction:
- For one organisational symptom, list competing causal hypotheses and the observations that would distinguish them.
Key point:
- Symptoms describe what is happening; causal explanations require additional evidence.
- Match the strength of the conclusion to the analytical design.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Analytical design
30. Q: How should analysts decide which variables and records are relevant to the refined problem?
A: Show answer
Select data because it helps answer a defined question, not simply because it is available or easy to measure. For each proposed field, explain its relationship to the outcome, the population, the period, and the comparison being made. Exclude information that cannot contribute to the question unless it is needed for a documented control, subgroup analysis, or alternative explanation. The chapter’s profitability example shows how breaking an issue into revenue and expense components can narrow the data required. The Government Data Quality Framework also cautions against measuring data characteristics that do not matter to the intended use. Keep a record of exclusions so that later reviewers can understand the scope of the analysis.
Study instruction:
- Create a variable-selection table with each field’s purpose, definition, source, expected quality, and reason for inclusion or exclusion.
Key point:
- Relevance is determined by the question and intended decision.
- Exclusions should be reasoned and documented, not arbitrary.
Source links:
- The Government Data Quality Framework
31. Q: How can a team decide whether a proposed sub-question is worth answering?
A: Show answer
Ask what result the sub-question could produce, which decision it would inform, who would use the answer, and whether the organisation could act on it. A technically interesting question may have little value if no stakeholder needs the result or if the answer cannot change a decision. Also assess the data required, likely effort, uncertainty, and whether the question duplicates another workstream. The chapter advises evaluating the expected outcomes of sub-questions before collecting more data. This creates a practical filter: prioritise questions that contribute to the central problem, defer low-value curiosity, and revise the plan if a key answer proves infeasible or irrelevant to the sponsor.
Study instruction:
- Score a list of proposed sub-questions against decision relevance, feasibility, expected value, effort, and duplication, then justify the priority order.
Key point:
- Do not spend resources answering questions nobody can use.
- Prioritisation should consider value and feasibility together.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Proportionality
32. Q: When is quantitative data collection appropriate for a data science question?
A: Show answer
Quantitative methods are appropriate when the question concerns counts, amounts, frequencies, measurable differences, distributions, or relationships that can be represented systematically. Examples include the number of delayed orders, salary changes, customer retention rates, or the proportion of users in a category. The chapter makes an important distinction: quantitative data is not limited to values that look like continuous numbers. Categorical responses such as preferred brand or political affiliation can also be counted. The method should be selected because it answers the question, not because numerical data is easier to store or analyse. Definitions, measurement rules, population, and sampling still need to be specified.
Study instruction:
- For five proposed questions, decide whether a count, measurement, category, or rate would answer each one and define the unit of observation.
Key point:
- Quantitative methods answer questions about measurable amounts, counts, and distributions.
- Categorical responses can be quantitative when they are systematically classified and counted.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: Evaluation methods
33. Q: When are qualitative methods more suitable than quantitative methods?
A: Show answer
Qualitative methods are useful when the analyst needs to understand experiences, reasoning, attitudes, motivations, perceptions, or the context behind behaviour. Open-ended interviews and other qualitative approaches can reveal why people interpret a process in a particular way or how an operational problem is experienced. They are especially helpful when the issue is poorly understood and the possible answers are not yet known. Their findings do not normally establish how common a view is across a wider population. The chapter therefore treats qualitative work as a way to gain context, not as a numerical substitute. Method choice should follow the question and the type of evidence needed.
Study instruction:
- Turn a question about customer dissatisfaction into an open-ended interview question and identify what a qualitative answer could and could not establish.
Key point:
- Qualitative methods explore meaning, experience, and context.
- Rich accounts do not by themselves measure population prevalence.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: Interview study—qualitative studies
34. Q: What are the strengths and limitations of combining quantitative and qualitative methods?
A: Show answer
A mixed-methods design can combine the breadth of numerical patterns with the depth of accounts explaining how or why those patterns arise. For example, a retention analysis may identify which customer groups leave at higher rates, while interviews explore the experiences and reasons reported by those customers. The approaches can complement each other, but they require more planning, analytical skill, time, and resources. Their findings may also disagree because they measure different things or involve different participants. The analyst should decide in advance how the evidence will be integrated and should not treat agreement between methods as automatic proof of causality.
Study instruction:
- Design a mixed-methods question and specify which part is answered quantitatively, which part qualitatively, and how the results will be compared.
Key point:
- Mixed methods can add depth and context while increasing complexity and cost.
- Plan how evidence from different methods will be integrated and interpreted.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: Mixed methods study
35. Q: How should interviews be designed when additional qualitative information is needed?
A: Show answer
Begin with a clear research question and a small set of topics that every interview should address. A semi-structured guide provides consistency while allowing follow-up questions when participants raise relevant experiences or explanations. Select participants for the perspectives needed, explain the purpose and use of the information, and consider how recordings, notes, and transcripts will be handled. The GOV.UK interview guidance notes that interviews can provide in-depth insight but are time-consuming and cannot establish how common each view is. Review material promptly, analyse themes systematically, and document the selection and limitations of the interview sample.
Study instruction:
- Draft a semi-structured guide with an opening explanation, core questions, neutral probes, consent considerations, and a plan for thematic analysis.
Key point:
- Interview design should follow the research question and participant context.
- Qualitative depth is not a substitute for a representative prevalence estimate.
Source links:
- GOV.UK: Interview study—qualitative studies
36. Q: What ethical issues should be considered before gathering additional data from people?
A: Show answer
The analyst should consider whether the proposed collection is necessary for the question, whether participants understand the purpose, and whether the information could expose them to harm or unwanted disclosure. Review privacy, confidentiality, access, retention, security, consent or other lawful basis as applicable, fairness in who is included, and the effect of the research on participants. Sensitive topics may require additional safeguards and specialist review. The UK Government Data and AI Ethics Framework emphasises responsible use, privacy, fairness, accountability, and protection from harm. These considerations should shape the design before collection begins, not be added after data has already been gathered.
Study instruction:
- Complete an ethics and privacy screening for a proposed interview or survey, documenting necessity, participant risks, access, retention, and safeguards.
Key point:
- Collect only information justified by the purpose and approved process.
- Ethical safeguards are part of project design and data governance.
Source links:
- UK Government: Data and AI Ethics Framework
37. Q: Why must the project team inform stakeholders of the refined problem before moving to data preparation?
A: Show answer
The team needs a shared understanding of what will be investigated, what will not be investigated, how the work will proceed, and what outputs are expected. Without this alignment, stakeholders may interpret the question differently, expect unplanned deliverables, or judge the final result against criteria that were never agreed. The chapter recommends explaining the parameters and approach to the relevant stakeholders after the preceding steps have been completed. The AQuA Book similarly treats engagement and scoping as a way to establish a common understanding of the problem and align expectations with what can be produced. Agreement should be revisited if material assumptions or constraints change.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a project-framing presentation and ask stakeholders to confirm the problem, scope, evidence needs, assumptions, and expected decisions.
Key point:
- Stakeholder alignment is a control against misunderstanding and rework.
- A shared problem statement should precede detailed preparation and analysis.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Engagement and scoping
38. Q: What should be documented in a written problem statement or project agreement?
A: Show answer
A useful record states the business issue, refined analytical questions, purpose and intended decisions, population and period, proposed outputs, key assumptions, known data constraints, stakeholders and responsibilities, milestones, and acceptance arrangements. It should identify exclusions and any important dependencies, such as access to systems or cooperation from another department. The chapter specifically recommends obtaining stakeholder agreement in writing before data preparation, because informal understandings can change as the project develops. The record need not be a large contract for every small task, but it should be proportionate to the risk and complexity and sufficiently clear to resolve later disputes about what was agreed.
Study instruction:
- Draft a concise project brief and test whether an independent reader could identify the question, boundaries, deliverables, owners, and approval route.
Key point:
- Written scope turns shared understanding into a referenceable commitment.
- Document assumptions and exclusions as well as deliverables.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Roles and responsibilities
39. Q: How can written stakeholder agreement help prevent scope creep?
A: Show answer
Scope creep occurs when additional requirements are introduced without a corresponding decision about time, resources, priorities, or the original objective. The chapter warns that stakeholders may change their expectations as they see the project develop. A written baseline makes the agreed question, outputs, timetable, and exclusions visible, so new requests can be assessed as changes rather than quietly absorbed. When a request arises, describe its value, effort, dependencies, and effect on existing commitments; then obtain an authorised decision to add, substitute, defer, or reject the work. A scope document does not prevent all change, but it makes change deliberate and traceable.
Study instruction:
- Create a change-request template that records the proposed addition, reason, impact on time and resources, trade-offs, approver, and decision.
Key point:
- New work should trigger an explicit scope decision.
- Written agreement helps distinguish approved change from uncontrolled expansion.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Engagement and scoping
40. Q: How should an analyst prepare stakeholders for results that may be unwelcome?
A: Show answer
Explain at the beginning that the purpose of analysis is to learn from evidence, not to confirm a preferred narrative. State that the result may support, qualify, or contradict the stakeholder’s expectation and that the method will be applied consistently regardless of the outcome. Agree how limitations and uncertainty will be communicated and who needs to be involved in interpreting the findings. If results are negative, present the evidence clearly, distinguish observation from explanation, and discuss practical implications without disguising the result.
