The chapter’s advice is a professional stance on honest analysis; it does not remove the need for tact, context, and appropriate review before conclusions are communicated.
Study instruction:
- Draft an opening statement for a project that explains analytical independence, possible outcomes, uncertainty, and how findings will be discussed.
Key point:
- Prepare stakeholders for evidence that may challenge expectations.
- Communicate unfavourable findings accurately and constructively.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Delivery, communication and sign-off
41. Q: What should an analyst do if a stakeholder pressures the team to make the findings more favourable?
A: Show answer
Separate legitimate requests to correct an error or add missing context from pressure to alter a result without evidence. Recheck the data, definitions, calculations, and assumptions; invite appropriate technical review; and document any correction with its rationale. If the evidence remains unchanged, report it faithfully and explain what it does and does not establish. Escalate attempts to suppress, distort, or selectively present material findings through the organisation’s governance or professional channels. The chapter says not to “pretty up” negative results to spare feelings; this should be applied with careful explanation rather than personal confrontation. Analytical integrity requires transparency about uncertainty as well as about adverse results.
Study instruction:
- Work through a scenario in which a sponsor disputes an unfavourable result and distinguish valid quality challenges from unsupported requests to change the conclusion.
Key point:
- Correct genuine errors, but do not change findings to satisfy expectations.
- Preserve an audit trail of review, decisions, and escalation.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Quality assurance and communication
42. Q: How should analysts manage time during the problem-identification stage?
A: Show answer
The chapter advises spending enough time to understand the issue, stakeholders, industry, data limitations, and scope, but not trying to eliminate every uncertainty before moving forward. This stage should normally be a relatively small part of the project cycle, although a complex problem involving many departments may require more effort. Use checkpoints to decide whether the problem is sufficiently defined to proceed, and record unresolved questions that can be addressed during later stages. Over-analysis can delay useful work, while under-analysis can cause expensive rework. The appropriate balance depends on project complexity, risk, deadline, and the cost of making a wrong decision.
Study instruction:
- Set entry and exit criteria for the scoping stage and identify which uncertainties must be resolved now versus monitored later.
Key point:
- Scope enough to proceed responsibly, not to achieve impossible certainty.
- Time spent upfront should be proportionate to complexity and risk.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Proportionality
43. Q: How should the chapter’s suggestion to add a 20 percent time allowance be interpreted?
A: Show answer
The author offers adding about 20 percent to an estimated project duration as a practical rule of thumb because data science work often encounters obstacles and deadlines can be difficult to meet. It is not presented as a universal project-management standard or a statistically validated buffer for every type of work. Teams should estimate effort using the project’s complexity, dependencies, access risks, review cycles, and uncertainty, then decide whether contingency is appropriate. The important principle is to avoid optimistic commitments that leave no room for foreseeable problems. Any contingency should be managed transparently and should not become an excuse for avoidable delay.
Study instruction:
- Estimate a project using task-level assumptions, then compare the estimate with and without contingency and explain the risks each plan carries.
Key point:
- The 20 percent figure is the chapter author’s heuristic, not a mandatory rule.
- Base contingency on project-specific uncertainty and dependencies.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Proportionality and analytical lifecycle
44. Q: What should an analyst do when a project may miss an agreed deadline?
A: Show answer
Communicate the risk early to the person who needs to know, explain the cause, describe the effect on the deliverable, and offer realistic options. Options may include reducing non-essential scope, changing the sequence, obtaining additional access or expertise, issuing a clearly labelled interim result, or revising the date. Do not wait until the deadline has passed if a material obstacle is already visible. The chapter links early communication with stakeholder trust and recommends avoiding commitments that cannot reasonably be met. The revised plan should be documented and approved by the appropriate decision-maker, especially when it affects other teams or external obligations.
Study instruction:
- Write a delay notice that states the cause, impact, options, recommended action, and decision required without hiding uncertainty.
Key point:
- Early warning gives stakeholders a chance to manage consequences.
- Revise scope or timing through an explicit decision, not silence.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Delivery and communication
45. Q: Why may a data scientist need to say no to a new project request?
A: Show answer
Capacity is limited, and accepting every request can displace work that has greater organisational value or jeopardise existing commitments. The chapter advises pausing before agreeing, evaluating the value of the proposed work, and considering whether the request fits the organisation’s priorities. The value need not be financial; it may involve customer outcomes, efficiency, risk reduction, or another stated objective. A decision should also consider effort, data access, dependencies, and whether the requester can act on the result. Saying no is not a rejection of the person; it is a prioritisation decision that protects the usefulness and quality of the work already undertaken.
Study instruction:
- Rank several competing requests by expected value, urgency, effort, feasibility, and strategic fit, then explain which work should be accepted or deferred.
Key point:
- Accepting every request can reduce value and delivery reliability.
- Prioritisation should be based on organisational benefit and capacity.
46. Q: How can an analyst decline a request while still helping the colleague who raised it?
A: Show answer
The chapter suggests positioning the data scientist as an adviser rather than only as the person who extracts numbers or completes every analysis. After considering the request, the analyst can explain why it is not a current priority and offer a limited alternative, such as clarifying the question, recommending an existing report, pointing to an appropriate tool, or helping the colleague identify what information they need. This preserves a constructive relationship while keeping ownership of the proposed work with the requesting team. Any advisory support should still have boundaries so that a declined project does not return as an unplanned commitment through repeated small requests.
Study instruction:
- Draft two responses to a low-priority request: one that declines the work clearly and another that offers a bounded advisory alternative.
Key point:
- A respectful refusal can still provide useful direction.
- Define the limits of any alternative support to avoid hidden scope expansion.
47. Q: What should an analyst do if a colleague deliberately withholds information needed for the project?
A: Show answer
First verify that the information is genuinely required, identify the owner, and establish whether the delay reflects a legitimate access, security, workload, or approval issue. Explain the project dependency and request a clear resolution date. If the blockage appears intentional or continues without a valid explanation, document the requests and their effects, then use the agreed management or governance route to escalate it. The chapter advises escalating deliberate stonewalling because the project exists to create organisational value. Escalation should remain proportionate, factual, and consistent with access controls; it does not authorise bypassing confidentiality or security requirements.
Study instruction:
- Prepare an escalation record showing the information needed, owner, requests made, responses, impact, and the decision required from management.
Key point:
- Resolve legitimate access barriers through the proper process.
- Escalate persistent obstruction with evidence and without bypassing controls.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Roles and responsibilities
48. Q: How can an analyst preserve useful exploratory discoveries without allowing the project to lose focus?
A: Show answer
Keep a separate record of observations that fall outside the agreed question, including the evidence seen, the possible business relevance, and the additional work needed to assess them. During project reviews, ask the sponsor whether any discovery changes the priority or requires a formal scope change. Do not quietly add new analyses simply because the data makes them easy to perform. If the discovery suggests a serious risk or urgent issue, follow the organisation’s escalation process rather than waiting for the final report. This approach respects the chapter’s encouragement to remain curious while maintaining a clear boundary between the commissioned question and new opportunities.
Study instruction:
- Review an exploratory findings log and decide which observations belong in the current project, which need escalation, and which should become future work.
Key point:
- Curiosity adds value when discoveries are recorded and assessed deliberately.
- A new insight does not automatically authorise new scope.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Analytical lifecycle
49. Q: What does the VSCO case study illustrate about refining an initially broad customer question?
A: Show answer
The case describes a data leader who began with a broad interest in who buys subscriptions and whether purchasing changes user behaviour. By discussing the issue with colleagues, he identified the marketing context and reformulated the work around how users could be segmented by preferences, behaviour, and demographics, and which segments were more likely to purchase. The revised questions were more useful because they connected the analysis to a specific business function and clarified what information was needed. The case illustrates the value of stakeholder discussion and question decomposition; it does not establish that any observed segment characteristic causes a purchase or that the same variables will predict behaviour in another organisation.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the case from initial issue to refined questions and identify the stakeholder knowledge needed at each step.
Key point:
- Contextual discussion can turn a broad curiosity into a decision-relevant investigation.
- Segmentation and prediction should not be confused with causal explanation.
50. Q: What is the integrated workflow for identifying and refining a data science question before data preparation?
A: Show answer
Begin by clarifying the issue and why it matters now. Speak with the requester, identify affected stakeholders and decision-makers, build relevant industry and process knowledge, and examine the organisation from both strategic and operational perspectives. Check what data exists, who owns it, what limitations may affect the question, and whether further collection is feasible. Decompose the issue into useful sub-questions, use exploratory data mining only as a source of leads, and choose quantitative, qualitative, or mixed methods according to the evidence required. Finally, agree the refined problem, scope, outputs, responsibilities, milestones, assumptions, and limitations in writing. The sequence is iterative: new evidence may justify a controlled revision, but the project should never lose sight of the decision it is intended to support.
Study instruction:
- Apply the complete workflow to a real organisational issue and produce a signed-off problem statement, stakeholder map, data-feasibility note, sub-question tree, and method rationale.
Key point:
- A strong project begins with a shared, feasible, decision-relevant question.
- Strategic context, operational evidence, data limitations, and stakeholder agreement must work together.
Source links:
- The AQuA Book: Government guidance on robust analysis
- The Government Data Quality Framework

Table 5
Data Preparation: ETL, Cleaning, and Quality Assurance
1. Q: What is data preparation, and why must it take place before analysis?
A: Show answer
Data preparation turns raw information into a form that people and analytical systems can interpret consistently. It includes extracting source data, identifying defects, correcting or managing inconsistencies, resolving or documenting gaps, standardizing formats, and checking the loaded result. Real organizational data is rarely collected under one standard: contributors may use different conventions, rename columns, omit values, or move files through systems that alter them. An algorithm cannot reliably infer which differences are harmless and which change the meaning of a record. Analysis should therefore begin only after the dataset is shown to be fit for the intended question. Preparation does not create new facts; it makes existing information usable and exposes what is not known.
Study instruction:
- "Map the source-to-analysis journey for a dataset you use and list the checks required before the first calculation or model is run."
Key point:
- Preparation makes data interpretable and fit for a defined purpose.
- An unexamined dataset can produce convincing but incorrect results.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
2. Q: What does data wrangling mean, and what is its practical objective?
A: Show answer
Data wrangling is another name for data preparation: discovering the structure and condition of raw data, then reshaping it so it can be reliably used. It may include consolidating files, renaming fields consistently, converting types, joining related tables, splitting combined fields, aggregating records, investigating unusual values, and recording how missing information was handled. The objective is not to make a dataset look neat at any cost. It is to make meaning, structure, and limitations clear enough that a later analysis answers the intended question without hidden distortion. A repeatable method is preferable to ad hoc fixes because it reduces rework and makes decisions reviewable.
Study instruction:
- "Take one messy spreadsheet and document each wrangling action, its reason, and whether it changes a value or only its representation."
Key point:
- Wrangling combines structural transformation with quality management.
- Every material change should have a reason and an audit trail.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
3. Q: How can a practitioner judge whether a dataset is sufficiently prepared?
A: Show answer
The chapter gives three readiness conditions: the data must be in the right format, free from errors, and have its gaps and anomalies accounted for. These conditions are evaluated relative to the analysis question. A date field may be correctly formatted but still contain impossible dates; a dataset may contain valid values but omit an entire customer group; and an unusual value may be genuine even though it is far from the typical range. Preparation is complete only when the practitioner can explain what was checked, what was corrected, what remains uncertain, and why those limitations do not invalidate the planned use—or why the analysis must be restricted.
Study instruction:
- "Write a preparation sign-off covering format, errors, missingness, anomalies, residual risks, and fitness for the intended analysis."
Key point:
- Correct format alone does not establish accuracy or completeness.
- Unresolved gaps and anomalies must be understood, not hidden.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework: data quality dimensions
4. Q: What does the principle garbage in, garbage out mean in data science?
A: Show answer
Garbage in, garbage out means that an analytical method cannot reliably rescue poor-quality inputs. Incorrect values, missing fields, inconsistent units, corrupted records, or misread dates can flow into calculations and models. The result may be a failed algorithm, an error such as division by zero, a distorted average, or a prediction that appears precise but reflects a data defect. The danger is not limited to obvious crashes: a model may run successfully while its output is materially misleading. Preparation is therefore a core part of the Data Science Process, not cosmetic tidying performed only when an error appears.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one analytical result and trace how an incorrect, missing, or misformatted input could change its conclusion."
Key point:
- A successful program run does not prove that the input was sound.
- Preventing data defects is safer than trying to interpret their effects later.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
5. Q: What are the three stages of ETL, and what happens at each stage?
A: Show answer
ETL means Extract, Transform, and Load. Extract obtains data from its current locations, ideally without changing the original records. Transform converts the extracted information into a structure and format suitable for the intended question and target system; this includes cleaning, standardizing, joining, splitting, and aggregating where justified. Load places the prepared result into the target store, such as a data warehouse, where it can be queried and analysed. The stages are connected: extraction choices affect what can be transformed, transformation rules affect the loaded structure, and loading can itself introduce truncation or conversion problems that require another check.
Study instruction:
- "Draw an ETL diagram for a project and identify the input, each transformation, the target store, and the evidence that each stage succeeded."
Key point:
- ETL is a pipeline, not a single import command.
- Quality checks are needed during and after all three stages.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
6. Q: Why does the chapter recommend cleaning data throughout the ETL process?
A: Show answer
Practitioners differ on whether cleaning should happen mainly before transformation or after loading. The chapter's author prefers checking and cleaning at each ETL stage because defects can be introduced, exposed, or amplified at different points. An extraction can misread a source format; a transformation can create incorrect joins or type conversions; and a load can truncate text or reinterpret values. Waiting until the end makes it harder to identify which operation caused the problem. Stage-level checks do not mean repeatedly changing the same value without control; they mean applying suitable tests at each boundary and recording the result.
Study instruction:
- "Assign quality tests to extraction, transformation, and loading, and specify the failure response for each."
Key point:
- Data quality can deteriorate at more than one pipeline stage.
- Stage-level checks make defects easier to isolate and correct.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
7. Q: Which source locations may need to be included during data extraction?
A: Show answer
The chapter lists databases, Excel spreadsheets, websites, social-media sources such as Twitter, CSV files, and paper reports. A project may also receive extracts from operational applications, survey platforms, finance systems, or partner organizations. Establish where information actually lives before deciding that the dataset is complete. When multiple sources are needed, identify their owners, update cycles, field definitions, and keys so that records can be consolidated without accidentally double-counting or losing context. A source inventory also helps reveal data that exists but has not yet been considered by the project team.
Study instruction:
- "Create a source register showing each system or file, its owner, fields, time coverage, refresh frequency, and extraction method."
Key point:
- Source discovery determines the boundaries of the evidence base.
- A source inventory can expose both missing inputs and duplicate collection.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
8. Q: Why should an analyst extract a working copy rather than analyse directly in the original source?
A: Show answer
The chapter advises extracting data even when it is stored in one location, because analysis performed inside the original storage facility can accidentally modify or damage the source. In a shared operational system, experimental queries or manual edits can affect other users, interrupt business processes, or compromise the only reliable copy. A controlled extract provides a stable basis for analysis while preserving original records for comparison and recovery. The extract should be identifiable by date, source, version, and extraction method. Access controls and approved procedures may still be necessary, especially when the source contains confidential or personal information.
Study instruction:
- "Define a read-only extraction procedure and verify that source counts and sample values remain unchanged after the extract is created."
Key point:
- Preserving the original protects reproducibility and operational continuity.
- A working extract should retain provenance back to its source.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
9. Q: What is a CSV file, and why is it commonly used for data exchange?
A: Show answer
CSV stands for comma-separated values. It is a plain-text representation in which each record normally occupies a new line and fields are separated by delimiters, commonly commas. Because CSV does not carry spreadsheet formatting in the same way as an Excel workbook, it can be imported by many different programs and is a common interchange format. Its simplicity does not guarantee correctness: delimiters inside text, quotation marks, encoding, line breaks, and missing fields must still be interpreted consistently. The receiving system needs to know the delimiter, text qualifier, encoding, and expected number of fields.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect a CSV in a text editor and compare its raw delimiters and line breaks with the table shown after import."
Key point:
- CSV improves portability by separating values from workbook presentation.
- Import settings still determine how text, delimiters, and missing fields are interpreted.
Source links:
- Microsoft Support: importing and exporting text or CSV files
10. Q: What is a relational database, and how does it connect separate datasets?
A: Show answer
A relational database stores information in tables whose relationships can be expressed through common fields. A shared field such as Country or a unique customer identifier can link records across tables, allowing an analyst to query related information together rather than repeatedly assembling separate extracts by hand. The relationship must be meaningful: matching two tables by a non-unique or inconsistently defined field can multiply records or attach information to the wrong entity. Relational design therefore depends on clear keys, consistent types, and known relationships between the entities represented by the tables.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch two related tables and identify the primary key in each and the field used to connect them."
Key point:
- Relational analysis depends on valid relationships between records.
- A shared column name alone does not prove that two fields have the same meaning.
Source links:
- PostgreSQL documentation: constraints and relational integrity
11. Q: What advantages does a relational database offer compared with using a single Excel workbook?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies three advantages. First, database columns have predefined data types, so incompatible values can be rejected instead of silently placed in a cell. Second, relational databases are designed to combine tables through common values and queries, whereas combining multiple Excel tables can require complex manual work or programming. Third, databases are designed to scale to much larger volumes of records, while spreadsheet performance and usability can deteriorate as data grows. Excel remains useful for quick calculations, exploration, and presentation, but it should not be assumed to provide the integrity or scale of a properly designed database.
Study instruction:
- "Compare one Excel workflow with a relational-database workflow and list where type checks, joins, and volume handling differ."
Key point:
- Databases can enforce structure and relationships centrally.
- Tool choice should follow data volume, integrity needs, and intended use.
Source links:
- PostgreSQL documentation: constraints and relational integrity
12. Q: How do database data types and constraints help prevent errors?
A: Show answer
A database schema defines the kinds of values expected in each field, and constraints can enforce additional rules. For example, a date field can reject text that cannot be interpreted as a date; NOT NULL can require a value; UNIQUE can prevent duplicate keys; FOREIGN KEY can require a referenced record to exist; and CHECK can enforce a row-level condition. These controls prevent some defects at the point of entry or loading. They do not prove that a value is true in the real world: a syntactically valid date may still be the wrong person's date of birth, and a unique identifier may still be incorrectly assigned. Constraints complement, rather than replace, source validation and human review.
Study instruction:
- "Design an appropriate constraint for a key field, a required field, and a numeric range in a sample table."
Key point:
- Schema rules enforce structural validity, not full factual accuracy.
- Constraints should reflect documented business rules and relationships.
Source links:
- PostgreSQL documentation: constraints
13. Q: What kinds of transformation can be performed before data is loaded?
A: Show answer
Transformation can include joining tables, splitting one field into several, aggregating detailed records, renaming or standardizing columns, converting data types, and cleaning invalid or inconsistent entries. Each transformation should be driven by the analytical objective. Joining tables can provide context, but the join key and expected record cardinality must be checked. Splitting a combined address may help analysis, but it can introduce parsing errors. Aggregation may reduce detail and make some later questions impossible. Keep the raw extract and record the transformation logic so that a reviewer can reproduce the prepared output and understand what detail was retained or removed.
Study instruction:
- "List each proposed transformation and state its purpose, expected row impact, and validation test."
Key point:
- Transformation changes structure or representation to serve a defined use.
- Unjustified transformations can destroy detail or alter relationships.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
14. Q: How should incorrect, corrupt, and missing data be distinguished?
A: Show answer
Incorrect data was entered wrongly or violates an understood fact or rule, such as a currency amount appearing in a date field or a city-country combination that cannot be correct. Corrupt data may once have been correct but has become mangled through file damage, software conversion, or an unsuitable extraction or transfer. Missing data means that a value is unavailable or was not entered. The distinctions matter because remedies differ: incorrect data may need source correction; corrupt data may need re-extraction or recovery; missing data may require investigation, documented retention, justified derivation, imputation, or exclusion. A single empty-looking cell should not be classified without understanding its origin.
Study instruction:
- "Classify five defects in a sample dataset and explain what evidence would confirm each classification."
Key point:
- Incorrect, corrupt, and missing values have different causes and remedies.
- Classification should precede any attempt to repair a record.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework: data quality dimensions
15. Q: How can cross-field inconsistencies reveal incorrect data?
A: Show answer
A field may look plausible in isolation but conflict with another field in the same record. The chapter's example pairs the country France with the city Rome; the mismatch indicates that at least one value needs investigation. Similar checks can compare a date of birth with an age, a transaction currency with an account currency, or a start date with an end date. Such tests should be based on documented domain rules and should distinguish hard impossibilities from unusual but valid combinations. A flagged inconsistency is an exception for review, not permission to replace a value automatically.
Study instruction:
- "Define three cross-field validation rules for your dataset and test them against both valid exceptions and clear errors."
Key point:
- Cross-field checks reveal errors that simple type checks cannot detect.
- A validation flag should trigger investigation when the correct value is not certain.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
16. Q: Why can missing CSV fields shift the interpretation of later records?
A: Show answer
When a CSV record contains an omitted field without the expected delimiter, the receiving program may infer fewer columns than the schema requires. Depending on the import process, later values can then be assigned to the wrong columns or rows, creating a cascading alignment problem. An empty field represented by adjacent delimiters is different from a delimiter that is absent altogether. Analysts should inspect raw text as well as the imported table, compare field counts with the expected schema, and investigate changes in row structure before running calculations.
Study instruction:
- "Find a CSV row with fewer delimiters than expected and demonstrate how its values would be misassigned under a fixed column schema."
Key point:
- Missing values and missing delimiters are not the same defect.
- Malformed records can shift fields and contaminate data below the original error.
Source links:
- Microsoft Support: importing and exporting text or CSV files
17. Q: Why does the chapter advise checking the bottom of a dataset as well as the top?
A: Show answer
The chapter warns against checking only the first 100 records. If a malformed row causes columns to shift, subsequent records may also be misinterpreted, so errors can be more apparent further down the file. Reviewing only the beginning gives a false sense of security because data-entry problems may occur late or be introduced during an append operation. A practical inspection should sample both ends, examine transitions between batches, compare row and column counts, and use automated checks across the full dataset wherever possible. Bottom-up inspection is a useful manual technique, not a substitute for comprehensive validation.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the top and bottom 100 rows of a file, then run a full-file field-count and type check to see which defects sampling misses."
Key point:
- Quality checks should cover the whole file or a defensible sample design.
- The bottom of a dataset can reveal cascading alignment and late-entry problems.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
18. Q: What should be done first when a record appears corrupt?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes three initial responses: re-extract the data from its original file to test whether the first extraction caused the damage; contact the person responsible for the data to clarify what the value should be; or exclude the affected row from the analysis if the problem cannot be resolved and the omission is methodologically acceptable. The order depends on source availability and operational context. Retain the defective extract, document the suspected corruption, compare re-extracted values, and avoid silently overwriting the evidence. Exclusion is not a universal fix: it can reduce sample size or bias the result.
Study instruction:
- "Plan a recovery sequence that preserves the original, re-extracts the source, consults the owner, and documents the final disposition."
Key point:
- Re-extraction tests whether damage occurred during transfer or import.
- Unrecoverable records should be excluded only with a documented analytical rationale.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
19. Q: How should an analyst request missing information from colleagues or data owners?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends approaching colleagues courteously and neutrally because data collection may be an unwelcome additional task for people whose main role is not analytics. Explain the question the project is trying to answer, identify the exact field or records that are missing, describe why the information matters, and make clear what the colleague is being asked to do. Showing the bigger picture can help the owner prioritize the request. Keep track of contacts, dates, responses, and unresolved gaps so that repeated requests remain consistent and the project has evidence of its collection process.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a concise data request specifying the missing field, affected records, purpose, requested evidence, deadline, and contact for clarification."
Key point:
- Good communication improves the chance of obtaining reliable missing data.
- A precise request is easier to action than a vague request for better data.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
20. Q: Why should an organization maintain an inventory of its data assets?
A: Show answer
A data-asset inventory helps analysts discover which information already exists and reduces the risk of requesting or collecting the same data again. The chapter recommends recording source names, database columns, and field descriptors. A useful inventory can also document the system owner, purpose, coverage, update frequency, access route, definitions, quality limitations, and relationships to other datasets. The inventory should be maintained as systems change; an old list can be misleading if a source is retired or a field changes meaning. It is a discovery aid, not a substitute for checking the actual current source.
Study instruction:
- "Create a small data catalogue and use it to check whether a proposed new collection duplicates information already held elsewhere."
Key point:
- A source catalogue reduces blind spots and duplicate collection.
- Field descriptions and ownership are as important as source names.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
21. Q: How should an analyst decide whether a missing value can be derived with certainty?
A: Show answer
A missing value can be filled from other fields only when the relationship is deterministic in the specific context and the supporting fields are reliable. The chapter's example is deriving Utah from Salt Lake City, but this works only where the city name is unambiguous. Profit can also be derived from revenue and expenses when both inputs are valid and their definitions are consistent. The key distinction is between a mathematically or logically certain derivation and an estimate. Record the rule, verify its assumptions, and retain an indicator that the value was derived rather than directly observed.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a missing field that might be derivable and prove that the mapping is unique for every relevant record before filling it."
Key point:
- Only fill a gap as a fact when the derivation is genuinely certain.
- A plausible guess is not equivalent to a verified source value.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework: data quality dimensions
22. Q: When is it reasonable to leave a missing value blank?
A: Show answer
Leaving a value blank may be appropriate when the field is not needed for the current analysis, when the method can properly handle missingness, or when filling the gap would require an unsupported assumption. The chapter recommends documenting where gaps remain so later anomalies can be interpreted. Blank does not mean zero, not applicable, unknown, or not collected; those states can have different meanings. Where possible, use an explicit missingness code or metadata convention that preserves the reason for absence, while ensuring that analytical software interprets it correctly. The decision should be made against the intended question, not convenience.
Study instruction:
- "For each missing field, distinguish unknown, not applicable, not collected, and withheld, and decide how each should be represented."
Key point:
- Leaving a value blank can be safer than inventing one.
- Missingness should be described so users do not mistake it for zero or not applicable.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
23. Q: When should a record with missing information be excluded from an analysis?
A: Show answer
A record may be excluded when a missing field is essential to the analysis and no defensible recovery or modelling approach is available. Removing a record reduces sample size and can reduce statistical significance; sample size alone does not make the decision safe. If missingness is concentrated in a particular group, period, or outcome, excluding those records may introduce selection bias and change the population being studied. Document the criteria, number and characteristics of records removed, and how exclusion affects the result.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate the number and proportion of records proposed for exclusion and compare key characteristics of retained and excluded groups."
Key point:
- Exclusion can change both sample size and representativeness.
- A row should not be removed solely because it is inconvenient.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework: completeness and accuracy
24. Q: When is mean or median imputation appropriate for a numerical field?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes replacing a missing numerical value with a mean or median as one possible method. The mean is the total divided by the number of observed values; the median is the middle value after sorting, or the average of the two middle values for an even count. The median is often less affected by extreme values, so it can be preferable for skewed data. Neither statistic is automatically appropriate: an imputed value reduces observed variability and can distort relationships, especially when many values are missing or missingness is systematic. The method should be justified, flagged, and tested for sensitivity.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate both mean and median for a skewed column, compare their effect on the distribution, and document why one is more defensible."
Key point:
- The median is less sensitive to extreme observations than the mean.
- Imputation creates an estimate and can affect downstream analysis.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework: completeness and accuracy
25. Q: Why should numeric identifiers not be treated as ordinary numerical measurements?
A: Show answer
Some fields contain digits but do not represent quantities. Customer numbers, postal codes, phone numbers, credit-card numbers, and inception years used as categories should be handled as identifiers or labels rather than values for arithmetic. Averaging two customer numbers or a company founding year does not produce a meaningful missing value. The chapter uses inception as an example: although it is numeric, it is not necessarily a measure for which the mean or median is meaningful. Correctly identifying the semantic type prevents invalid imputation and can also prevent software from changing the field's format.
Study instruction:
- "Classify every numeric-looking field as a measure, identifier, date/time, code, or category before deciding how it should be cleaned or imputed."
Key point:
- Data type should reflect meaning, not merely the characters in a cell.
- An identifier can be numeric in appearance but categorical in use.
Source links:
- Microsoft Support: keeping leading zeros and large numbers
26. Q: What is model-based imputation, and what caution should accompany it?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes using correlations and similarities in observed records to estimate missing numerical values, giving K-nearest neighbours as an example that is developed further in a later chapter. A model can identify records with similar characteristics and use their observed values to estimate a gap. This can be more tailored than replacing every gap with one overall average, but it relies on the choice of features, distance measure, scaling, and assumptions about which records are comparable. The estimate is not recovered truth. It should be flagged, validated, and compared with alternative assumptions, especially when the imputed field is important to the final conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "Identify candidate predictor fields for one missing numerical variable and explain how you would test whether the modelled estimates are credible."
Key point:
- Modelled values remain estimates, even when generated by an algorithm.
- The imputation method can introduce its own bias or error.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework: accuracy and completeness
27. Q: What is a missingness indicator, and why can it be useful?
A: Show answer
A missingness indicator is an additional variable that records whether the original value was present or missing, often coded yes/no. The chapter suggests adding such a flag so an analyst can examine whether missingness is associated with other variables. This can reveal patterns—for example, if a particular survey group is less likely to answer a question or if records from one source lack a particular field. The indicator does not repair the original gap and does not explain why the value is missing. It preserves information about the absence, which may itself be analytically important.
Study instruction:
- "Create a missingness flag for a field and compare its distribution across relevant groups, dates, and source systems."
Key point:
- The fact that a value is missing may carry information.
- A missingness flag helps analyse patterns without pretending to know the absent value.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
28. Q: How does the venture-capital case study illustrate that missing-data treatment depends on field meaning?
A: Show answer
The chapter's fictional venture-capital dataset has missing values across employees, industry, inception year, state, revenue, expenses, profit, and growth. Employees are numerical, so the industry median may be a useful proxy if assumptions are acceptable. Industry can potentially be researched; if it is essential and cannot be established, the record may need to be excluded. Inception year is a date-like category rather than a quantity to average. State can be derived only where the city-to-state mapping is certain. Expenses can be calculated from revenue and profit if those values are reliable. Where several financial values are missing, the chapter proposes estimating revenue and expenses with industry medians and then recomputing profit for internal consistency.
Study instruction:
- "For each field in the case study, classify it and choose a distinct treatment, explaining which choices are certain and which are estimates."
Key point:
- Missing-value treatment must follow the field's meaning and role.
- Related fields should be checked for logical and mathematical consistency.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
29. Q: How should dependent numerical fields be reconciled after imputation?
A: Show answer
When one field is derived from others, filling each gap independently can create contradictions. The chapter's example uses revenue, expenses, and profit: where revenue and expenses are estimated, profit should then be recalculated as revenue minus expenses rather than independently imputed to a value that may not reconcile. The same principle applies to totals, subtotals, ratios, and growth measures. First identify the underlying variables and equations, then decide which observed or estimated values are sufficiently reliable to serve as inputs. Keep original values and imputation flags so a reviewer can distinguish observed figures from derived results.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a dependency diagram for totals, subtotals, and ratios in your dataset and identify the order in which missing fields should be resolved."
Key point:
- Dependent fields should reconcile to their underlying components.
- Recalculation can preserve internal consistency better than independent filling.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
30. Q: What is an outlier, and why does it need investigation?
A: Show answer
An outlier is an observation that lies unusually far from the pattern or range of other values in a dataset. It may be the result of a data-entry error, a unit mismatch, a corrupted value, or a genuine unusual event. The chapter's example is a distributor's telephone number mistakenly entered as the number of product units sold; if included in an average, it would dominate the result. The analyst should investigate the source, surrounding records, units, and business context before deciding whether the value is wrong. An unusual value is a signal to examine, not proof of an error.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the most extreme values in a numerical field and trace each to its source record before changing or excluding it."
Key point:
- Outliers can materially distort summaries and models.
- Extreme does not automatically mean incorrect.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework: accuracy and validity
31. Q: How can an analyst distinguish an erroneous outlier from a legitimate extreme observation?
A: Show answer
An erroneous outlier conflicts with source evidence, field definitions, units, or a plausible data-entry process; a legitimate extreme observation is unusual but supported by reliable evidence. Check original documents, transaction records, neighbouring fields, data-entry logs, and the data owner's explanation. A very large purchase order, for example, may be genuine even if it is far above typical orders. If the value is valid, preserve it and choose an analytical method suited to the question. If it is an error, correct it from an authoritative source or document exclusion. Do not delete values only because they make a chart or average look inconvenient.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a case note for one extreme record, classifying it as erroneous, valid, or unresolved and listing the evidence supporting that conclusion."
Key point:
- The source and context determine whether an extreme value is erroneous.
- Valid exceptional events should not be erased simply to make data look normal.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
32. Q: How can a distribution curve help identify outliers?
A: Show answer
The chapter suggests plotting a distribution curve, or a bell curve when data follows a normal distribution, to see where values fall relative to the main concentration. Values far from the peak or in the tails can then be investigated. A chart is a diagnostic aid, not a decision rule: the distribution may be skewed or multimodal, and a rare value may be meaningful. The analyst should first understand the variable, units, and population, then combine visual inspection with appropriate statistical summaries and source checks. Do not assume every variable should form a normal bell curve.
Study instruction:
- "Plot the distribution of a relevant variable, identify tail observations, and investigate whether the shape reflects error, skew, or real subgroups."
Key point:
- Visual distributions help direct investigation but do not prove a record is wrong.
- The expected shape depends on the variable and population.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework: validity and accuracy
33. Q: When might a valid outlier be excluded from a particular analysis?
A: Show answer
The chapter gives the example of a publishing house analysing average books sold to retailers. An unusually large but genuine exceptional order may be removed from that particular calculation if the question is intended to describe typical routine sales. The exclusion is an analytical choice, not a correction of the source record. It should be stated explicitly, supported by the purpose of the analysis, and accompanied by a comparison showing how the result changes with and without the exceptional event. The record should remain available in the source dataset and may be relevant to a different question, such as peak demand or total sales.
Study instruction:
- "Recalculate one summary with and without a verified extreme event and explain which result answers the stated business question."
Key point:
- A valid outlier may be excluded for a specific analytical purpose, not deleted from history.
- Transparent sensitivity analysis shows the effect of the decision.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework: accuracy
34. Q: What Excel automatic-conversion problems can damage data during extraction?
A: Show answer
The chapter warns that Excel may change long numerical strings into scientific notation and may reinterpret dates or currency according to regional settings. Identifiers such as account numbers, phone numbers, and credit-card numbers are not quantities and must not be treated as ordinary numeric measures. Microsoft guidance explains that Excel can remove leading zeros and retain only 15 digits of precision when long numeric strings are converted to numbers; digits beyond that precision can be lost. Formatting a damaged number as text afterward does not restore the original digits. Protect such columns as text before or during import.
Study instruction:
- "Test an extract containing leading zeros, a 16-plus-digit identifier, and a date string, then verify the imported characters against the raw source."
Key point:
- Automatic conversion can irreversibly change identifiers.
- Set the intended column type at import rather than repairing after precision loss.
Source links:
- Microsoft Support: keeping leading zeros and large numbers
35. Q: How should long identifiers and values with leading zeros be protected in Excel?
A: Show answer
Treat identifiers as text before import or entry. Microsoft describes importing text through Power Query and assigning the relevant column a Text type, and provides automatic-conversion settings that can preserve leading zeros and long numeric strings. A display format alone is not always sufficient: if Excel has already converted a 16-digit identifier to a number, digits may have been rounded and cannot be restored simply by changing the cell format. Validate the imported result against the original file, especially for account codes, product codes, postal codes, telephone numbers, and other values whose digits are identifiers rather than quantities.
Study instruction:
- "Import a sample file through Power Query, explicitly set identifier columns to Text, and compare the results character by character with the source."
Key point:
- Preserve identifiers as text from the beginning of the import process.
- A display format cannot recover digits that have already been lost.
Source links:
- Microsoft Support: Excel data import options
36. Q: Why are date formats especially vulnerable to regional settings?
A: Show answer
Date strings can use day-month-year or month-day-year order, and the same sequence of digits may therefore mean different dates in different locales. The chapter contrasts a common dd/mm/yyyy convention with the US mm/dd/yyyy convention. If an import process assumes the wrong locale, a valid date may be interpreted as a different date or rejected as impossible. A consistent representation such as yyyy-mm-dd reduces ambiguity when values are exchanged, but the original text still needs to be parsed under its correct source locale. A formatted display is not proof that the underlying date was interpreted correctly.
Study instruction:
- "Take a sample of ambiguous dates and explain which locale is required to parse each one before converting it to a standard date value."
Key point:
- Date parsing requires knowledge of the source convention.
- An unambiguous display format does not correct a date that was parsed incorrectly.
Source links:
- Microsoft Learn: data types and locale in Power Query
37. Q: How should dates be standardized during a data-preparation workflow?
A: Show answer
First identify the source date convention and parse the text using the correct locale. Then convert the resulting value into a consistent date type and choose a standard representation for exchange, such as yyyy-mm-dd, as recommended by the chapter. In Power Query, the Using locale option lets the analyst specify the locale used to interpret text during type conversion. Verify the result by extracting or checking year, month, and day components and comparing a sample with the original source. Do not simply change a cell's display format when the underlying date may already have been misread.
Study instruction:
- "Build a date-conversion test that includes unambiguous dates, ambiguous dates, invalid dates, and dates near month or year boundaries."
Key point:
- Parse with the source locale before applying a common output format.
- Verify converted date components against the original evidence.
Source links:
- Microsoft Learn: Using locale for data type conversion
38. Q: Why must currency symbols and decimal separators be handled carefully?
A: Show answer
Currency values may contain symbols, thousands separators, and decimal marks that vary by country. A string such as 1.234,56 can mean something different from 1,234.56 depending on locale. If a symbol or separator is treated incorrectly, a numeric amount may be imported as text or interpreted at the wrong magnitude. The chapter recommends stripping symbols and standardizing separators, but the safe implementation is to parse the value under the correct locale first, preserve currency as a separate attribute, and then store a numeric amount using a canonical representation. Never remove punctuation blindly before identifying which characters are decimal marks and which are grouping separators.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare two currency examples from different locales and show the correct parsed amount and separate currency code for each."
Key point:
- Locale determines how decimal and thousands separators are interpreted.
- Currency identity should not be lost when symbols are removed from a numeric field.
Source links:
- Microsoft Learn: data types and locale in Power Query
39. Q: What is the risk of treating a currency symbol as part of the numeric value?
A: Show answer
A currency symbol embedded in a text field can prevent software from recognizing the amount as a number, making arithmetic and aggregation unreliable. Removing the symbol without retaining currency identity can create a different problem: a value of 100 dollars and a value of 100 euros would become indistinguishable. Store the numeric amount separately from its currency code, and convert between currencies only when the analysis has a defined exchange-rate basis and date. Decimal and thousands separators must also be interpreted using the correct locale before conversion. The output should preserve the amount and the context needed to understand it.
Study instruction:
- "Design a data structure with separate amount, currency-code, transaction-date, and exchange-rate fields, and test it with mixed currencies."
Key point:
- Numeric parsing and currency identity are separate data-quality requirements.
- Never discard information needed to interpret or compare financial amounts.
Source links:
- Microsoft Learn: data types and locale in Power Query
40. Q: How should data be loaded into a warehouse after transformation?
A: Show answer
Load the transformed data into the target warehouse only after its structure and types match the intended schema. The load process should define destination tables, field mappings, keys, and treatment of nulls, text qualifiers, and duplicates. Loading is not the end of preparation: the destination may reject values, coerce types, truncate long text, or create unexpected row changes. Keep a record of the input extract, transformation version, load time, and result counts so errors can be traced. The chapter calls for one final manual review before the data is passed to an algorithm.
Study instruction:
- "Document the destination schema and run a controlled test load with row-count, type, key, and text-length checks before loading the full dataset."
Key point:
- Loading can introduce defects even when transformation was successful.
- A successful load is not a substitute for post-load validation.
Source links:
- PostgreSQL documentation: constraints and data integrity
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
41. Q: Why must row counts be compared before and after loading?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends comparing the number of rows in the final dataset with the initial dataset. A difference can indicate rejected records, duplicated rows, filtering, parsing errors, or an unintended join or aggregation. The count alone cannot explain the cause, so reconcile the difference at record level and classify each removed, added, or changed row. Some pipelines are expected to change counts, for example when aggregating transactions to monthly totals; in that case the expected transformation should be documented and the count comparison should use the correct grain. An unexplained difference is a stop condition, not a rounding issue.
Study instruction:
- "Create a reconciliation that explains every row-count difference between the extracted dataset and the loaded result."
Key point:
- Row counts are a basic control for detecting unintended record loss or growth.
- Expected row changes must be documented by the transformation design.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
42. Q: What post-load checks should be performed on the first and last records?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends checking the top 100 and bottom 100 rows and reviewing columns for skewness or unexpected patterns. These checks can reveal import problems concentrated at the beginning or end of a file, appended batches with different conventions, or records shifted by missing delimiters. A practical review should compare the first and last records with the source, test the first and last dates, inspect extreme numeric values, and confirm that all fields remain aligned. Sampling is useful for manual review, but automated checks should cover all rows wherever possible.
Study instruction:
- "Build a post-load checklist for the first and last 100 records and add automated rules that test the full dataset."
Key point:
- Boundary checks can expose defects caused by late records and appended batches.
- Sampling should supplement full-dataset validation, not replace it.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
43. Q: Why should dates and balances receive special post-load attention?
A: Show answer
The chapter singles out dates and balances as fields especially susceptible to corruption. Dates can be reinterpreted through locale settings or converted to text; balances may be affected by decimal separators, signs, truncation, or an incorrect numeric scale. A post-load check should compare selected records with the original source, confirm data types, verify minimum and maximum values, inspect sign and precision, and reconcile totals where an independent control exists. For financial balances, also preserve currency and reporting-period context. The aim is to detect a technically successful load that has changed the meaning of the information.
Study instruction:
- "Select date and balance fields and specify independent checks for parsing, sign, scale, period, currency, and reconciliation."
Key point:
- Fields vulnerable to conversion errors need targeted checks.
- A valid data type does not prove that a date or balance retained its original meaning.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
44. Q: How can a database load truncate free-form text or mishandle quotation marks?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that database columns may impose a maximum text length. If a survey response exceeds that limit, the stored value may be cut off, creating missing information or affecting downstream processing. Free-form text can also contain quotation marks and other characters that have special meaning in a delimited file or import format. The preparation process should establish appropriate destination lengths, use a correct text qualifier and escaping convention, check encoding, and compare stored text length and content with the source. A sample check is not enough when truncation affects only rare long responses; automated length comparisons are preferable.
Study instruction:
- "Load a text sample containing very long responses, quotation marks, commas, and non-ASCII characters, then compare source and destination content."
Key point:
- Text fields need both adequate length and correct escaping.
- A loaded record can be incomplete even when the row itself is present.
Source links:
- Microsoft Support: importing text and CSV data
45. Q: What role does peer review play in data-preparation quality assurance?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes quality assurance as a critical final stage and notes that consulting practices often require peer review. A reviewer should be able to see what source data was used, which transformations were applied, what exceptions remain, and whether the final numbers reconcile and make sense. Review is not intended merely to find fault; it protects later analysis and business decisions from avoidable errors. Organizations may set predetermined QA procedures and require evidence that each check was performed. The reviewer should be independent enough to challenge assumptions and should document unresolved concerns before the dataset is released.
Study instruction:
- "Create a peer-review checklist and ask a colleague to reproduce one transformation and independently validate its output."
Key point:
- Independent review challenges assumptions that the preparer may overlook.
- QA evidence should show both the checks performed and their outcome.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
46. Q: How should an organization define data-quality rules for a particular project?
A: Show answer
Quality rules should follow the purpose of the dataset rather than a generic demand that every field be complete. The Government Data Quality Framework describes dimensions such as completeness, uniqueness, consistency, timeliness, validity, and accuracy. For a customer analysis, uniqueness of customer records may be critical; for an operational forecast, timeliness may matter more; for a survey, a field may legitimately be not applicable to some respondents. Define thresholds and exception handling before analysis, explain remaining limitations to users, and make an owner responsible for improving recurring problems. The chapter's three readiness conditions provide a useful starting point, while the framework makes checks more specific.
Study instruction:
- "For a real project, rank relevant quality dimensions by importance and set a measurable rule and exception owner for each."
Key point:
- Data quality is fitness for purpose, not a single universal score.
- Users should be told about material gaps, limitations, and known inconsistencies.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework: six quality dimensions
47. Q: How can a repeatable data-preparation framework reduce time and errors?
A: Show answer
The chapter warns that an undocumented, improvised approach forces analysts to reinvent the process for each project. A repeatable framework defines source discovery, extraction, transformation rules, quality checks, ownership, exception handling, and release criteria. Reuse does not mean blindly applying the same assumptions to every dataset: field meanings, missingness, and business context still need to be assessed for each use. The framework should version its code and rules, preserve raw inputs, keep decision logs, and make it possible for another analyst to reproduce the prepared output. This reduces avoidable rework and makes the process easier to review.
Study instruction:
- "Turn a recent preparation task into a standard operating procedure with reusable checks and explicit project-specific decision points."
Key point:
- Standardization reduces repeated effort and inconsistent treatment.
- Project-specific assumptions must still be reviewed rather than copied blindly.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
- PostgreSQL documentation: constraints
48. Q: What does the Ubisoft case demonstrate about the strategic value of data preparation?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Ulf Morys, Finance Director at Ubisoft's German operation, drawing attention to data already gathered by the production team about online players and their behaviour. By mapping available data and identifying gaps, the finance team could ask better questions about stores, customers, purchasing patterns, and consumer attitudes. The example illustrates that data preparation is not merely a technical cleaning task: it helps an organization understand what information exists, what is missing, and whether it is suitable for a business decision. The cited podcast also describes Morys's earlier work using data analysis to reduce an acquisition valuation by about €40 million; this is an attributed case example, not a general promise of savings.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the case as a sequence of business question, existing data, identified gaps, new collection, preparation, analysis, and decision."
Key point:
- Data preparation can unlock value in information that already exists.
- Business questions reveal what missing data must be collected.
Source links:
- SuperDataScience podcast: Ulf Morys and data science at Ubisoft
49. Q: What should be documented when data is excluded, corrected, derived, or imputed?
A: Show answer
Record the affected fields and records, the original value or defect where retention is permitted, the reason for the action, the evidence consulted, the rule or method applied, the person responsible, and the date and version of the change. Distinguish a verified correction from a deterministic derivation and from a statistical estimate. For exclusions, state the selection criteria and the number and characteristics of records affected. For imputation, preserve a flag and the method used. This documentation lets reviewers understand whether the result depends on a judgement, an estimate, or a confirmed source value, and supports sensitivity analysis if assumptions change.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one example of each action—correction, derivation, imputation, and exclusion—and create a record of the evidence and decision for each."
Key point:
- A preparation log preserves the rationale for material decisions.
- Corrections, derivations, estimates, and exclusions should not be presented as equivalent.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
50. Q: How should an analyst decide whether to proceed to analysis or return to preparation?
A: Show answer
Proceed only when the dataset meets the agreed quality criteria for the question, all high-impact exceptions have been resolved or explicitly accepted, and the loaded output has passed its checks. Return to preparation when counts do not reconcile, a key field has an unexplained type or locale problem, missingness is material and undocumented, text has been truncated, or an extreme value has not been investigated. The decision should also consider whether the data covers the population and period required by the question. A clean-looking warehouse is not enough; the analyst must be able to explain its limitations and why they are acceptable for the next stage.
Study instruction:
- "Write a go/no-go decision for a dataset using measurable readiness tests, named reviewers, and documented residual risks."
Key point:
- Analysis should begin only after important preparation defects are controlled.
- A release decision must account for both data quality and analytical purpose.
Source links:
- Government Data Quality Framework guidance
- Government Data Quality Framework: data quality dimensions
