Table 6
Data Analysis: Classification, Clustering, and Predictive Modelling
1. Q: What is the central purpose of Chapter 6, “Data Analysis Part 1,” in Confident Data Skills?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains how practitioners move from understanding a data problem and preparing its data to applying analytical algorithms that discover or predict patterns. It emphasizes the context and intuition of methods rather than requiring readers to derive every mathematical formula. Its main focus is classification, where predefined categories are predicted, and clustering, where groups are discovered from data. Within those themes it introduces decision trees, random forests, k-nearest neighbours, Bayes and Naive Bayes, logistic regression, K-means, and hierarchical clustering. The practical goal is to understand what a method does, what information it needs, how its output should be interpreted, and where its limitations matter.
Study instruction:
- "Map each named algorithm to classification or clustering and summarize the chapter's purpose in three sentences."
Key point:
- Analysis answers a clear question using prepared data
- classification predicts known labels, while clustering discovers structure.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
- scikit-learn: Clustering
2. Q: Where does the analysis stage sit within the wider data science process?
A: Show answer
The chapter positions analysis after the practitioner has understood the task and prepared the relevant data. Problem definition determines what decision or uncertainty the analysis must address; preparation makes observations and variables usable; analysis applies methods to identify patterns or make predictions. These stages depend on one another. A sophisticated algorithm cannot repair a poorly framed question, a target that does not represent the intended outcome, or data inconsistent with the task. Analysis brings a defensible question and usable dataset together to produce evidence that can inform a decision.
Study instruction:
- "Describe a real task in the order: business question, target or discovery goal, preparation, analysis method, decision supported."
Key point:
- Analysis depends on a well-understood task and prepared data
- algorithm choice cannot compensate for a badly framed problem.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Cross-validation
3. Q: Why does the chapter emphasize understanding algorithms intuitively rather than deriving all their mathematics?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that useful professional understanding does not always require reconstructing the mathematics behind a model. Its analogy is that a person can operate a car after learning the controls without being able to dismantle the engine. In data work, practitioners need to know the inputs, the logic behind predictions, the meaning of outputs, and conditions in which a method may fail. Mathematical detail remains valuable when developing methods, diagnosing behavior, or assessing assumptions, but it is not a prerequisite for every applied task. Intuition must be accurate: it should be a reliable mental model, not a shortcut that hides uncertainty.
Study instruction:
- "Explain one algorithm in plain language, then identify one mathematical assumption a deeper technical review would investigate."
Key point:
- Practical intuition makes analytical methods accessible
- it must clarify assumptions rather than conceal them.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
- scikit-learn: Naive Bayes
4. Q: What is the difference between classification and clustering?
A: Show answer
Classification assigns observations to categories already defined for the task. The model learns from examples whose categories are known, then predicts a category for a new observation. Clustering groups observations by similarity when useful group structure is not specified in advance. A yes-or-no survey outcome or predicting whether an airline customer will churn is classification because the target categories are known. Grouping customers by income and spending patterns, when the number and nature of useful groups are unknown, is clustering. The distinction concerns the question and the availability of target labels, not whether data are displayed as points or charts.
Study instruction:
- "Classify five proposed analyses and justify each by stating whether target categories are defined before modelling."
Key point:
- Classification predicts known target classes
- clustering explores structure without predefined labels.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
- scikit-learn: Clustering
5. Q: What are features, target labels, and observations in a classification dataset?
A: Show answer
An observation is one case being analysed, such as a customer, survey respondent, transaction, or wine harvest. Features are recorded characteristics used to describe that case, such as age, flight frequency, spending, rainfall, or service usage. The target label is the known category the model is trained to predict, such as churn or no churn, yes or no, winner or loser. Features are inputs; the label represents the outcome. A feature should not reveal the answer in a way unavailable at prediction time. The analyst must verify that labels are consistent and correspond to the decision the model is meant to support.
Study instruction:
- "For airline churn, list the observation unit, five plausible features, the target label, and one possible source of leakage."
Key point:
- Features describe each case
- the target is the category to predict. Labels must be consistent and genuinely observed.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
- scikit-learn: Cross-validation
6. Q: Why does classification normally require historical examples with known outcomes?
A: Show answer
A supervised classifier needs examples connecting input characteristics to the categories it must learn to predict. Historical cases provide both feature values and observed labels, allowing the algorithm to identify patterns associated with each class. An airline cannot train a useful churn classifier merely from customer ages and routes if it has no reliable record of which customers subsequently stopped using the service. Labels may come from operational records, surveys, or defined business events. Incorrect, delayed, inconsistent, or biased labels can make patterns misleading. Historical examples should also resemble the conditions in which future predictions will be used.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect a proposed training dataset and verify how each target label was established and whether the definition is consistent."
Key point:
- Supervised classification learns from known outcomes
- poor labels teach the model the wrong relationship.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
- scikit-learn: Cross-validation
7. Q: How should an analyst define the classification target before choosing an algorithm?
A: Show answer
The target should represent a specific outcome that matters to the decision, have categories that can be distinguished consistently, and be observable in available records. Define the observation unit, event being predicted, time horizon, and rule for assigning each class. In a churn task, “stopped using the airline” should become an operational definition, such as an agreed period without a booking, rather than a subjective label. The target should not combine unrelated outcomes merely for convenience. Define it before reviewing model results so categories are not changed to make performance appear stronger.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a target definition for churn or a survey task, including unit, event, period, and ambiguous-case rules."
Key point:
- A useful classifier begins with a precise, decision-relevant target
- ambiguity undermines training and evaluation.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
- scikit-learn: Cross-validation
8. Q: What is a decision tree and how does it classify an observation?
A: Show answer
A decision tree represents a sequence of questions about input features. The first question is at the root; depending on the answer, an observation follows a branch to another question or eventually reaches a terminal leaf. Each leaf represents a predicted class or distribution over classes. The model learns splitting rules from labelled training examples so cases with similar outcomes are routed toward the same leaves. A new customer is classified by following the learned rules using that customer's feature values. The flowchart-like structure makes the decision path comparatively easy to inspect.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a small tree for a binary outcome and trace two observations from the root to their predicted leaves."
Key point:
- A tree applies sequential feature-based rules
- a terminal leaf provides the prediction or class probabilities.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
9. Q: What are the root, internal nodes, branches, and leaves in a decision tree?
A: Show answer
The root is the first node and contains the initial split applied to all training observations. Internal nodes contain further tests on features after earlier tests have narrowed the cases. Branches represent possible outcomes of a test and determine the route an observation follows. Leaves are terminal nodes: no further split is made, and the model provides its prediction there. This vocabulary helps an analyst read a tree as a decision process. A deep tree has many conditions and can describe detailed patterns, but excessive depth can make it harder to interpret and fit peculiarities in the training data.
Study instruction:
- "Label the root, internal nodes, branches, and leaves on a tree diagram and explain each test."
Key point:
- Nodes and branches describe the prediction path
- leaves are where the model produces its output.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
10. Q: How does a decision tree choose where to split the data?
A: Show answer
A tree-learning procedure evaluates candidate feature-based splits and selects one that improves separation of target classes under a chosen impurity or split criterion. A useful split creates child groups more homogeneous in class composition than the parent group. Further splits are applied to the resulting groups, subject to stopping rules and model settings. The chapter explains the process visually, like a flowchart that repeatedly divides information into smaller decisions. Exact criteria vary across implementations, so an analyst should check which method is used. A split is useful only when it improves prediction beyond the training examples.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the class composition of a parent group and proposed child groups and explain whether the split improves separation."
Key point:
- Splits aim to form child groups with clearer class composition
- criteria and stopping settings shape the result.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
11. Q: Why are decision trees often compared with flowcharts?
A: Show answer
Both structures express a process as conditional steps. A flowchart describes a human-designed procedure; a decision tree learns conditions from example data and uses them to predict. The analogy helps a non-specialist follow how an observation moves through tests such as whether a feature is above or below a threshold. It also lets analysts show which recorded characteristics led to a particular leaf. The analogy has limits: a learned tree reflects statistical patterns in historical observations, not necessarily a causal or policy-approved rule. Its branches should be checked for data quality, fairness, and operational appropriateness before they justify consequential actions.
Study instruction:
- "Explain a learned tree using a flowchart, then state why its rules are not automatically causal or policy rules."
Key point:
- The flowchart analogy improves accessibility
- learned associations are not automatically causal or acceptable decisions.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
12. Q: How can a decision tree help investigate an airline customer churn problem?
A: Show answer
The analyst defines churn and assembles historical customer cases with reliable churn or retention labels. Candidate features may include flight frequency, routes, travel class, inflight services, baggage requests, membership tier, and recent activity changes. The tree learns splits that separate customers with different observed churn outcomes. Its paths can identify combinations of characteristics associated with higher or lower predicted churn. The analyst should check whether patterns are stable, whether variables were available before the prediction date, and whether the proposed response is appropriate. A path may identify a predictive segment, but it does not prove that changing one feature will prevent a customer from leaving.
Study instruction:
- "Design a churn analysis plan with a defined outcome date, pre-outcome features, and a review process for segments."
Key point:
- Tree paths reveal characteristics associated with churn
- prediction does not establish cause.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
- scikit-learn: Cross-validation
13. Q: What makes a decision tree relatively easy to interpret?
A: Show answer
A tree can be visualized as explicit conditions, so an analyst can often explain a prediction by showing the tests followed from root to leaf. This is useful when stakeholders need to understand which features influenced a classification. It can also reveal unexpected rules, such as a feature appearing important because of a coding error or unusual subgroup. Interpretability should not be overstated: a large tree may have many branches or reflect unstable splits in limited data. The displayed route explains how the model produced a prediction; it does not validate the dataset, establish causation, or show the prediction is accurate enough for use.
Study instruction:
- "Select one prediction and write a plain-language explanation of its complete route and feature values."
Key point:
- A tree exposes an explicit sequence of rules
- an understandable path does not prove correctness or fairness.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
14. Q: What are the risks of allowing a decision tree to grow too deep?
A: Show answer
A very deep tree can create highly specific rules that fit irregularities and noise in the training observations. It may perform impressively on data it has already seen but predict poorly for new cases. Its many branches can make explanations cumbersome and fragile: small changes in the training sample may produce a different sequence of splits. Depth constraints, minimum observations per leaf, or pruning can limit unnecessary complexity. These settings should be selected using validation rather than chosen simply because a shallower tree looks neater. Compare performance and interpretability on data not used to fit the final tree.
Study instruction:
- "Compare shallow and deep trees on separate training and validation data, noting fit, complexity, stability, and new-case performance."
Key point:
- Excessive tree complexity can overfit
- complexity controls must be evaluated on unseen data.

- scikit-learn: Decision Trees

15. Q: What is the practical sequence for building and applying a decision tree classifier?
A: Show answer
Start by defining the target and observation unit, then select appropriate features available at prediction time. Prepare consistent labelled examples, separate data for fitting and evaluation, and fit the tree using documented split and complexity settings. Inspect the resulting tree for sensible rules, class imbalance, data errors, and overly specific branches. Assess performance on held-out cases and in the context of the decision being supported. After approval, use it to classify new observations and monitor predictions as the population and operating conditions change. The chapter's flowchart analogy explains mechanics; evaluation determines dependability.
Study instruction:
- "Create a checklist covering target definition, features, preparation, fitting, inspection, evaluation, deployment, and monitoring."
Key point:
- Tree modelling is a process, not a single command
- evaluation and monitoring continue after fitting.

- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
- scikit-learn: Cross-validation
16. Q: What is a random forest and how does it extend a decision tree?
A: Show answer
A random forest is an ensemble of many decision trees rather than a single tree. The trees are built with controlled randomness in training samples and, commonly, in the features considered when choosing splits. Each tree produces a prediction; the forest combines those outputs through voting or averaging class probabilities. Several trees are therefore used instead of relying on one set of splits. The combined model can reduce the instability of an individual tree, although the collection is less straightforward to explain as one path. It remains supervised when trained to predict known class labels.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how one customer record is evaluated by multiple trees and how their predictions become one forest prediction."
Key point:
- A random forest combines multiple trees
- it can be more stable but less directly interpretable than a small tree.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Ensembles and Random Forests
17. Q: Why can combining many decision trees improve predictive reliability?
A: Show answer
Individual trees can be sensitive to changes in the sample and may produce different predictions when learned splits change. A forest creates diversity among trees and combines their outputs, reducing the influence of an unusual split in one tree. When trees make partly different errors, aggregation can lower variance and improve generalization. This is not guaranteed: if all trees share systematic bias, poor labels, or flawed features, averaging will not correct the underlying problem. The ensemble also adds computation and makes the full reasoning path more complex. Compare it with simpler alternatives using the same evaluation design.
Study instruction:
- "Describe how several trees could make different errors and explain how aggregation may reduce the effect of one unstable tree."
Key point:
- Diverse models can reduce ensemble variance
- aggregation does not remove shared bias or poor data quality.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Ensembles and Random Forests
- scikit-learn: Cross-validation
18. Q: What is bootstrap sampling in a random forest?
A: Show answer
Bootstrap sampling draws a training sample with replacement from the available training observations. An observation may appear more than once in one tree's sample while another may be omitted from that sample. Repeating the process produces different samples for different trees and creates variation among them. This is one source of ensemble diversity. Bootstrap sampling does not allow the forest to train on held-out test data; resampling must occur within designated training data. Analysts should understand how an implementation handles samples and whether out-of-bag observations are used for supplementary assessment.
Study instruction:
- "Use a small list of observations to demonstrate sampling with replacement and show how two bootstrap samples differ."
Key point:
- Bootstrap samples draw training cases with replacement
- evaluation data must not leak into model fitting.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Ensembles and Random Forests
19. Q: Why does a random forest consider random subsets of features when growing its trees?
A: Show answer
Random feature selection makes it less likely that every tree follows the same strongest feature at every split. This encourages differences among trees and can reduce correlated prediction errors. Randomness in observations and candidate features work together to create a collection of models whose predictions can be combined. The number of features considered at a split is a model setting that affects diversity and individual tree strength. Too little randomness may leave trees highly similar; too much may make individual trees weak. The appropriate configuration depends on the data and should be tested rather than assumed.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how restricting candidate features at each split changes tree diversity and identify the relevant model setting."
Key point:
- Feature subsampling is a deliberate source of diversity
- its amount is a tuning choice, not a universal constant.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Ensembles and Random Forests
20. Q: How does a random forest combine individual tree predictions?
A: Show answer
For classification, each tree can produce a class prediction or probabilities over classes. The forest aggregates these outputs. With hard voting, the class receiving the most votes is selected; with probability averaging, the class with the highest averaged probability is chosen. Software implementations may use one rule rather than another, so the analyst should check the method. The aggregate prediction is usually more stable than relying on one tree, but it can hide disagreement among trees. Class probabilities or prediction variation can help identify cases that deserve additional review.
Study instruction:
- "Trace one case through five trees, record their predicted classes, and calculate a majority-vote result."
Key point:
- A forest aggregates outputs from its trees
- confirm whether the implementation uses votes or averaged probabilities.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Ensembles and Random Forests
21. Q: What are the strengths and limitations of random forests compared with a single decision tree?
A: Show answer
A random forest can capture nonlinear relationships and interactions while reducing sensitivity to the training sample. It can provide strong predictive performance without requiring an analyst to design a long sequence of rules manually. However, it uses more computation, is less transparent than a small tree, and may reproduce bias in the training data. Aggregated predictions can be difficult to explain to a stakeholder who needs the exact rule behind a decision. A forest is attractive when predictive performance matters and a useful explanation is available; a small tree may be preferable when a compact decision path is central. Both need representative data and independent evaluation.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a tree and a forest using performance, interpretability, computational cost, and operational consequences."
Key point:
- Forests trade some interpretability for ensemble robustness
- the best model depends on the decision context.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
- scikit-learn: Ensembles and Random Forests
22. Q: What is k-nearest neighbours classification?
A: Show answer
K-nearest neighbours, usually abbreviated K-NN, classifies a new observation by finding the k training observations closest to it under a chosen distance measure. Their labels are used to determine the predicted class, commonly through a majority vote. Unlike a decision tree, K-NN does not usually build a compact sequence of rules during training; it retains examples and compares a new case with them when making a prediction. The chapter uses the intuitive idea of locating a new point among known examples. Results depend on the distance definition, feature scales, k, and distribution of stored examples.
Study instruction:
- "Plot labelled training points and a new point, identify the five closest observations, and determine the majority class."
Key point:
- K-NN predicts from nearby labelled examples
- distance and k shape its result.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Nearest Neighbors
23. Q: How does a K-NN classifier make a prediction for a new point?
A: Show answer
The fitted training examples are retained with feature values and known labels. For a new case, the algorithm computes distances to the training examples, ranks those distances, and selects the k nearest neighbours. It combines their labels, usually by majority vote, to predict the class. If neighbours are evenly divided, a tie-breaking rule or model configuration determines the result. Some implementations weight each neighbour by distance so closer cases contribute more strongly. The analyst should confirm the distance metric, feature processing, k value, voting rule, and tie behavior used.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate distances from a new point, rank the cases, select k, and tally the neighbour labels."
Key point:
- K-NN is a local prediction based on nearby cases
- its distance metric and voting rule must be documented.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Nearest Neighbors
24. Q: How should an analyst choose the value of k in K-NN?
A: Show answer
The value of k determines how many nearby examples contribute to a prediction. A small k makes the prediction sensitive to very local observations and can be affected by noise or a mislabeled case. A larger k smooths the local decision and may reduce the influence of noisy examples, but can blur distinctions between nearby groups and favor a broader majority. The chapter notes that k=5 is common and odd values can help avoid ties in binary classification; this is not a universal optimum. The best value depends on data, class distribution, distance measure, and error costs. Select it with validation procedures, not by repeatedly inspecting the final test results.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate candidate k values on validation data and compare performance, stability, and class-specific errors rather than choosing by convention."
Key point:
- Small k is locally sensitive
- larger k smooths more broadly. The best k is data-dependent.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Nearest Neighbors
- scikit-learn: Cross-validation
25. Q: Why does the chapter recommend considering odd values of k for binary K-NN classification?
A: Show answer
When a binary classifier uses an even number of neighbours, the nearest cases can be split equally between the two classes. An odd k makes an exact tie in a simple two-class majority vote less likely, although it does not eliminate every ambiguity in multiclass or weighted settings. The chapter describes k=5 as a common starting point partly because an odd number can avoid a two-versus-two tie when k=4. This is a practical convenience, not a guarantee that an odd k produces a better model. The value still needs assessment against data, class imbalance, validation results, and tie-handling rules.
Study instruction:
- "Create a four-neighbour example that produces a tie, compare it with five neighbours, and explain the limitation."
Key point:
- Odd k can reduce equal-vote ties in binary majority voting
- it does not replace empirical selection.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Nearest Neighbors
26. Q: What is Euclidean distance and why is it used in K-NN?
A: Show answer
Euclidean distance measures straight-line separation between observations represented as points in a feature space. With two features, it is the square root of the sum of squared differences in the two coordinates; the principle extends across more dimensions. K-NN uses distance to decide which stored examples are most similar to a new case. Euclidean distance is intuitive and common, but treats each coordinate according to its numeric scale and the geometry of the space. If one feature has a much larger range than another, it may dominate the distance even when it is not more important to the problem.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate Euclidean distance between two two-feature cases, then extend the calculation to a third feature."
Key point:
- Euclidean distance is a geometric measure of separation
- its meaning depends on appropriate feature scales.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Nearest Neighbors
27. Q: Why should features often be scaled before applying Euclidean-distance K-NN?
A: Show answer
K-NN treats numeric differences as part of the distance calculation. If one feature is measured in thousands while another ranges from zero to one, the large-scale feature can dominate and determine which observations count as neighbours. Scaling places variables on comparable ranges or reflects intended weights. Scaling parameters must be learned from training data and applied consistently to validation, test, and live observations; calculating them using all data can leak evaluation information. Scaling is not automatically appropriate for every variable: categorical features need suitable encoding and distance treatment, and domain knowledge should guide weighting.
Study instruction:
- "Compare neighbour rankings before and after scaling features with different units; derive scaling parameters only from training data."
Key point:
- Unscaled features can distort similarity
- fit preprocessing on training data and reuse it consistently.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Nearest Neighbors
- scikit-learn: Cross-validation
28. Q: What is the curse of dimensionality for K-NN?
A: Show answer
As feature dimensions increase, observations can become sparse in the feature space and the contrast between nearest and more distant cases can weaken. Distance-based neighbourhoods may then be less informative because even the closest training examples are far away or differ along many coordinates. K-NN may require substantially more observations to represent the space adequately, while noisy or irrelevant features worsen the problem. The chapter highlights multidimensional spaces and the limitation of nearest-neighbour methods in high-dimensional settings. Analysts can select relevant features, reduce dimensionality where justified, and evaluate whether distance still reflects meaningful closeness.
Study instruction:
- "Describe how adding irrelevant variables changes a K-NN problem's geometry and identify a feature-selection response."
Key point:
- High-dimensional spaces can weaken distance comparisons
- relevant features and adequate data coverage are essential.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Nearest Neighbors
29. Q: What is the difference between uniform and distance-weighted voting in K-NN?
A: Show answer
Uniform voting gives every selected neighbour equal influence when determining the predicted class. Distance-weighted voting gives closer neighbours more influence, reducing the contribution of observations near the edge of the selected neighbourhood. Weighting can help when local proximity is a strong signal and very close cases should count more. It can also be sensitive to duplicates, measurement errors, or near-zero distances, so implementation details matter. The choice should be evaluated alongside k and the distance metric. It is not enough to assume a numerically closer point is substantively more similar if the features are poorly chosen.
Study instruction:
- "Use one neighbour set to calculate a uniform vote and a distance-weighted vote, then explain a possible difference."
Key point:
- Uniform voting treats neighbours equally
- distance weighting emphasizes proximity but does not fix poor features.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Nearest Neighbors
30. Q: What are the main advantages and disadvantages of K-NN?
A: Show answer
K-NN is accessible, can represent nonlinear class boundaries, and requires little model-fitting work because it retains training examples. It can be useful when observations with similar feature values tend to share outcomes and the dataset is manageable. Weaknesses include prediction cost as the stored data grows, sensitivity to feature scaling and irrelevant variables, dependence on k and distance settings, and reduced effectiveness in high-dimensional spaces. It can also be difficult to explain why many features make two cases “near”. Operational use requires careful treatment of missing values, new categories, duplicates, and population changes. Select K-NN when its similarity assumption is credible and testable.
Study instruction:
- "Assess K-NN for a real application using data volume, feature quality, response time, interpretability, and population stability."
Key point:
- K-NN is flexible but relies on meaningful distances
- its data and prediction costs rise with stored sample size.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Nearest Neighbors
31. Q: What does Bayes’ theorem explain in classification?
A: Show answer
Bayes’ theorem describes how a probability estimate should be updated when new evidence is observed. It combines a prior probability for a hypothesis or class with the likelihood of the evidence under that class, then normalizes by the overall probability of the evidence. In classification, the hypothesis may be a class such as a positive or negative condition, while the evidence consists of observed features or a test result. The chapter's breathalyser example shows why a positive test must be interpreted in light of underlying prevalence and test behavior. The probability of evidence given a condition is not the same as the probability of the condition given evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Write Bayes’ theorem in words and label the prior, likelihood, evidence, and posterior in a screening example."
Key point:
- Bayes’ theorem updates prior belief using evidence
- conditional probabilities must not be reversed.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Naive Bayes
32. Q: What are prior probability, likelihood, evidence, and posterior probability?
A: Show answer
The prior is the probability assigned to a class before new evidence is considered. The likelihood is the probability of observing the evidence if that class were true. The evidence, sometimes called the marginal probability of the observations, is the overall probability of seeing the evidence across possible classes. The posterior is the updated probability of the class after evidence is considered. In a screening illustration, prevalence contributes to the prior, the positive rate among affected people contributes to the likelihood, and the overall positive rate contributes to the denominator. Keeping these components distinct prevents the mistake of treating sensitivity as the probability that a positive individual has the condition.
Study instruction:
- "Label each component of Bayes’ theorem in the breathalyser example and state what each probability is conditional on."
Key point:
- Posterior probability combines prevalence and likelihood
- every probability must be read with its condition stated.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Naive Bayes
33. Q: Why can a positive test result still correspond to a low probability that the person has the condition?
A: Show answer
When a condition is uncommon, unaffected people may greatly outnumber affected people. Even a test with a low false-positive rate can therefore produce more false positives from the large unaffected group than true positives from the small affected group. Bayes’ theorem accounts for this base-rate effect by combining prevalence with sensitivity and specificity. The chapter's breathalyser example illustrates why a positive result should not be interpreted without considering how common the condition is and how often the test is positive among people who do not have it. The calculation does not make tests useless; it shows why all relevant probabilities and appropriate follow-up evidence matter.
Study instruction:
- "Build a count table using stated prevalence, sensitivity, and specificity, then calculate the share of positive results that are true positives."
Key point:
- Low prevalence can make false positives important even with a good test
- predictive value depends on base rate and test quality.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Naive Bayes
34. Q: How should an analyst distinguish a false positive from a false negative?
A: Show answer
A false positive occurs when a model or test predicts that a condition is present although the true outcome is negative. A false negative occurs when it predicts that the condition is absent although the true outcome is positive. These errors have different consequences: a false positive may trigger unnecessary intervention, while a false negative may allow a genuine risk or need to go unnoticed. In the chapter's breathalyser example, a positive result for someone not over the relevant threshold is a false positive; a negative result for someone over it is a false negative. The relative cost of each error should influence how probabilities are interpreted and how a decision threshold is set.
Study instruction:
- "Construct a confusion matrix for a binary test and identify each error and its practical consequence."
Key point:
- False positives and false negatives are different prediction mistakes
- relative error costs influence decisions.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Naive Bayes
- scikit-learn: Logistic Regression
35. Q: What is Naive Bayes classification?
A: Show answer
Naive Bayes is a family of supervised classification methods built on Bayes’ theorem. It estimates the probability of each possible class given observed features and selects the class with the strongest posterior estimate or score. Its “naive” assumption is that features are conditionally independent of one another given the class. This simplification makes the joint likelihood easier to calculate because feature likelihoods can be combined rather than estimating every possible interaction. The assumption is often not literally true, so its effect on performance must be evaluated. The method demonstrates how probability can support classification without requiring a highly complex model.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how labelled training data supply class priors and feature likelihoods for Naive Bayes."
Key point:
- Naive Bayes applies Bayes’ theorem to predict class membership
- it simplifies calculation through conditional independence.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Naive Bayes
36. Q: What does conditional independence mean in Naive Bayes?
A: Show answer
The assumption means that once the class is known, the model treats each feature as providing evidence independently of the others. In a simplified prediction of wine-harvest success, the model may combine the likelihood of sunlight and rainfall under winner and loser classes as if those measurements were conditionally independent given the outcome. In reality, rainfall and sunlight may be related, so this is an approximation rather than a universal truth. Naive Bayes remains attractive because this simplification reduces the amount of data needed to estimate complex joint distributions. Analysts should understand the assumption and test whether predictions are useful for their dataset.
Study instruction:
- "Identify two potentially related features in a real problem and discuss how dependence could affect Naive Bayes."
Key point:
- Naive Bayes assumes conditional independence given the class
- the assumption is a simplification to evaluate.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Naive Bayes
37. Q: How does Naive Bayes combine evidence from multiple features?
A: Show answer
For each possible class, the method starts with the class prior and combines it with the likelihood of observed feature values under that class. Under conditional independence, the joint feature likelihood is approximated by multiplying individual conditional likelihoods. The resulting values are compared across classes, and the class with the greatest posterior score is selected. A probability denominator common to all classes may be omitted when only ranking is needed, although it is required for normalized posterior probabilities. The calculation depends on how likelihoods are estimated, which varies with feature type and the chosen Naive Bayes variant.
Study instruction:
- "For two classes and two features, write each unnormalized class score as prior multiplied by both conditional likelihoods and compare the scores."
Key point:
- Class priors and feature likelihoods jointly determine the score
- multiplying feature likelihoods follows the independence approximation.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Naive Bayes
38. Q: How does the chapter’s wine-harvest example illustrate Naive Bayes classification?
A: Show answer
The example uses conditions such as hours of sunlight and rainfall to distinguish harvests labelled winners or losers. Historical observations provide class labels and feature values associated with each outcome. The analyst estimates how likely sunlight and rainfall patterns are under each class, combines those likelihoods with the prior frequency of winning and losing harvests, and compares the resulting scores for a new case. The example shows how several pieces of evidence can be combined without deriving every probability formula from first principles. It also highlights the importance of representative historical examples and careful interpretation of the independence assumption.
Study instruction:
- "Recreate a small winner/loser table with sunlight and rainfall features and explain how class-specific likelihoods are estimated."
Key point:
- Naive Bayes converts feature patterns into class scores
- results depend on historical data and assumptions.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Naive Bayes
39. Q: What is the difference between Bayes’ theorem and the Naive Bayes classifier?
A: Show answer
Bayes’ theorem is a general probability identity for updating beliefs in light of evidence. It does not prescribe one machine-learning model or require feature independence. Naive Bayes applies the theorem while simplifying feature-likelihood calculations through conditional independence given the class. The theorem can be used with richer probability models that represent dependencies; Naive Bayes deliberately makes a stronger assumption to keep estimation tractable. Confusing the two can lead to the mistaken belief that conditional independence is required by Bayesian reasoning itself. It is the simplifying modelling assumption of the Naive Bayes family.
Study instruction:
- "Explain which part of Naive Bayes comes from Bayes’ theorem and which part is the added assumption."
Key point:
- Bayes’ theorem is general
- Naive Bayes is one model based on it. Conditional independence is an added assumption.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Naive Bayes
40. Q: What are the main limitations of Naive Bayes classification?
A: Show answer
Conditional independence can be unrealistic when features interact or contain overlapping information. Probability estimates can also be affected by sparse data, rare categories, poor class definitions, and the likelihood model chosen for numeric or categorical features. If class prevalence changes between training and deployment, predictions may be poorly calibrated. Some variants need smoothing or other handling for feature values that did not appear in training. The method may still rank classes effectively even when probabilities are imperfect, but this distinction must be checked. Analysts should inspect assumptions, evaluate performance, and consider the consequences of the decision.
Study instruction:
- "List three potentially dependent features in a real problem and explain how to test whether Naive Bayes remains useful."
Key point:
- Independence assumptions and sparse data affect results
- predictive usefulness and probability calibration are distinct checks.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Naive Bayes
- scikit-learn: Cross-validation
41. Q: What is logistic regression and why is it used for classification?
A: Show answer
Logistic regression estimates the probability that an observation belongs to a class, commonly in a binary setting. It forms a weighted combination of features and passes that value through a logistic or sigmoid function, mapping the result to a probability between zero and one. A decision rule can then convert the estimated probability into a class label. Despite the word “regression” in its name, it is commonly used as a classifier when the target is categorical. The chapter introduces it as a way to move from a straight-line model to a curve suitable for class probabilities. It still requires appropriate features, evaluation, and interpretation.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why logistic regression is a classifier and draw the relationship between linear score, sigmoid curve, and binary prediction."
Key point:
- Logistic regression estimates class probabilities using a logistic function
- the common task is classification.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Logistic Regression
42. Q: How does logistic regression differ from ordinary linear regression?
A: Show answer
Ordinary linear regression predicts a numeric outcome on an unbounded scale, such as an amount or measurement. Logistic regression models the probability of a categorical outcome by transforming a linear score through the sigmoid function, producing a value between zero and one. A decision threshold can then assign a class. The chapter uses a binary response such as whether a person opened an email to show why a straight line can produce values outside the probability range, while a logistic curve maps output into a valid interval. The methods address different target variables and should not be interchanged just because both use linear combinations of features.
Study instruction:
- "Use a numeric target and a yes/no target to decide whether linear or logistic regression is appropriate."
Key point:
- Linear regression predicts numeric values
- logistic regression estimates class probabilities within zero and one.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Logistic Regression
43. Q: What role does the sigmoid function play in logistic regression?
A: Show answer
The sigmoid converts a linear score into a smooth S-shaped value between zero and one. When the score is very negative, the predicted probability approaches zero; when it is very positive, it approaches one; around the middle, the output changes more rapidly. This lets logistic regression represent how combinations of features relate to the probability of a binary outcome. The model estimates coefficients and an intercept that determine the linear score before transformation. The resulting probability is not a guarantee about an individual case; it is an estimate whose quality depends on training data, assumptions, and calibration. The threshold for a class decision is separate.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch the sigmoid curve and mark low, medium, and high probabilities, distinguishing probability from the final class label."
Key point:
- The sigmoid maps a linear score to the zero-to-one range
- probability and threshold-based class are separate outputs.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Logistic Regression
44. Q: How does a logistic-regression threshold convert probability into a class label?
A: Show answer
The model first estimates a probability for the positive class. A threshold assigns the positive class when that estimate meets the threshold and the negative class otherwise. A threshold of 0.5 is common but is not automatically the best operational choice. Lowering the threshold generally identifies more positive cases, often increasing sensitivity while also increasing false positives; raising it usually reduces positive predictions but may miss more true positives. The correct threshold depends on error consequences, follow-up capacity, and probability quality. It should be chosen using validation evidence and an explicit rationale, not simply because 0.5 is familiar.
Study instruction:
- "Compare class assignments at thresholds 0.3, 0.5, and 0.7 and explain changes in error types."
Key point:
- A threshold turns probability into an operational class decision
- the best value depends on costs and capacity.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Logistic Regression
- scikit-learn: Cross-validation
45. Q: Why should predicted probabilities not be treated as certain outcomes?
A: Show answer
A model probability summarizes an estimate based on training-data patterns and model assumptions. A prediction of 0.8 does not mean the outcome is guaranteed; it means the model assigns a high estimated probability under its fitted relationship. The actual outcome can still be negative, and estimates may be systematically too high or low if data are unrepresentative or conditions have changed. Probabilities should be assessed for calibration and used with a suitable decision rule. Important decisions may require additional evidence or human review. Reports should distinguish predicted probability, assigned class, and observed outcome when it becomes known.
Study instruction:
- "Write a report that separately states predicted probability, threshold-based prediction, and eventual observed outcome."
Key point:
- A predicted probability expresses uncertainty
- its quality depends on calibration, data relevance, and assumptions.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Logistic Regression
- scikit-learn: Cross-validation
46. Q: How should an analyst compare the classifiers introduced in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The methods use different forms of evidence. A decision tree learns sequential rules; a random forest aggregates many randomized trees; K-NN examines nearby labelled examples; Naive Bayes combines class priors and feature likelihoods under conditional independence; logistic regression estimates class probabilities through a sigmoid transformation of a linear score. Data requirements, interpretability, computational costs, assumptions, and failure modes differ. No classifier is universally best. Define the target, create a fair evaluation design, compare error patterns and operating thresholds, and consider what explanations users need. A slightly lower score may be preferable if a method is easier to govern, maintain, or explain.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a comparison matrix for the five classifiers covering learning logic, assumptions, strengths, weaknesses, and example uses."
Key point:
- Classifiers encode similarity and decision logic differently
- selection should reflect the problem and operating requirements.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
- scikit-learn: Ensembles and Random Forests
- scikit-learn: Nearest Neighbors
- scikit-learn: Naive Bayes
- scikit-learn: Logistic Regression
47. Q: What is clustering and when should an analyst use it?
A: Show answer
Clustering groups observations according to similarity in recorded features without being given final group labels in advance. It is appropriate when the analyst wants to explore whether data contain useful segments or patterns not specified beforehand. The chapter illustrates this with survey respondents and customer data such as annual income and spending score: groups emerge from the observed distribution rather than fixed yes-or-no categories. Clustering can support segmentation and hypothesis generation, but resulting groups are mathematical structures that require interpretation. The analyst must determine whether clusters are meaningful, stable, and useful for the decision.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a dataset with no known target label and write an exploratory question that clustering could help investigate."
Key point:
- Clustering discovers groups from feature similarity without predefined labels
- the analyst must interpret the result.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
48. Q: Why is clustering described as an unsupervised learning method?
A: Show answer
Clustering is unsupervised because it is not trained against a supplied target label stating the correct group for every observation. It uses chosen features and a similarity or distance definition to organize observations. The analyst may want to understand customer types but does not specify exact membership the algorithm must reproduce. This distinguishes clustering from supervised classification, where known labels guide the model. Absence of a target does not mean the analysis is free of human choices: feature selection, scaling, distance metrics, algorithm settings, and interpretation all influence the clusters.
Study instruction:
- "Contrast a labelled churn dataset with an unlabelled segmentation dataset and identify the learning type for each."
Key point:
- Unsupervised clustering finds structure without target labels
- analyst choices still shape representation and meaning.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
49. Q: How does the customer-segmentation example use annual income and spending score?
A: Show answer
The example represents each customer as a point using annual income and spending score. Customers with similar values may appear near one another in this two-dimensional feature space, suggesting potential segments. A clustering algorithm organizes observations into groups that can be interpreted as customer profiles. The variables should be defined consistently and should support the business question. A label such as Cluster 1 is only an algorithmic identifier; it does not automatically mean “premium,” “low value,” or another category. The analyst must examine the members before assigning business language or recommending action.
Study instruction:
- "Sketch a customer scatter plot with income and spending score, then describe how each cluster should be profiled."
Key point:
- Feature values determine the geometry of segments
- cluster names require interpretation rather than assumption.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
- scikit-learn: K-Means
50. Q: What is K-means clustering?
A: Show answer
K-means is a centroid-based clustering algorithm that partitions observations into a specified number of groups. Each group is represented by a centroid, the mean position of observations assigned to that cluster. The algorithm assigns each point to the closest centroid, recalculates centroids from the new memberships, and repeats until assignments or the objective stop changing materially. The chapter explains the method visually with customers represented by income and spending score. K-means is useful when data can reasonably be represented by distance around central points, but it requires a chosen number of clusters and is sensitive to scaling, initialization, and cluster shape.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the K-means loop: initialize centroids, assign points, recalculate means, and repeat."
Key point:
- K-means alternates assignments and centroid updates
- K must be specified before running it.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
- scikit-learn: K-Means
51. Q: What is a centroid in K-means and how is it calculated?
A: Show answer
A centroid is the coordinate-wise mean of observations assigned to a cluster. In a two-feature example, its first coordinate is the average of the first feature values and its second coordinate is the average of the second feature values. The centroid represents the cluster's central location; it need not correspond to an actual observation. After assignments change, the algorithm recalculates the mean, so the centroid moves as membership changes. Because it depends on the scale and meaning of features, the analyst should ensure that the coordinates provide a reasonable measure of similarity.
Study instruction:
- "Calculate the centroid of three two-dimensional customer points by averaging each coordinate."
Key point:
- A centroid is the mean feature vector of a cluster
- it may not be an actual case.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
- scikit-learn: K-Means
52. Q: What are the main steps in the K-means algorithm?
A: Show answer
First choose the number of clusters and initialize that many centroids. Next calculate each observation's distance to the centroids and assign it to the nearest one. Recalculate each centroid as the mean of the assigned observations. Repeat assignment and update until memberships or centroid positions stabilize according to the stopping rule. The output includes a cluster label for each observation and final centroid coordinates. Results can depend on starting centroids, so different initializations may produce different solutions. Analysts often run the procedure multiple times and compare objective values and business usefulness.
Study instruction:
- "Write an operational checklist showing initialization, reassignment, centroid update, and stopping."
Key point:
- K-means alternates assignment and centroid updating
- initialization and stopping rules influence results.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
- scikit-learn: K-Means
53. Q: Why must the number of clusters be selected before running standard K-means?
A: Show answer
Standard K-means requires K because it must create that many centroids and partition observations around them. It optimizes assignments for the chosen K; it does not independently determine the correct number of segments. Selecting K is therefore a modelling decision informed by exploratory analysis, business needs, compactness, stability, and interpretability. A very small K can combine distinct populations; a very large K can fragment meaningful groups. The elbow approach can help explore the trade-off, but no single plot or statistic replaces judgment about whether groups are useful.
Study instruction:
- "Compare candidate K values and record the effect on compactness and interpretation."
Key point:
- K is an input to standard K-means
- cluster count should balance fit and practical usefulness.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
- scikit-learn: K-Means
54. Q: What is within-cluster sum of squares and what does it tell an analyst?
A: Show answer
Within-cluster sum of squares (WCSS) adds squared distances from observations to the centroid of their assigned cluster. It measures how tightly observations are grouped around their centroids under the chosen distance representation. Lower WCSS indicates points lie closer to their centers, but it does not automatically mean the clustering is better for the business question. WCSS generally decreases as more clusters are introduced because additional centroids can fit the data more closely. It is useful for comparing candidate K values and understanding the trade-off between compactness and complexity, not for selecting the largest possible number of groups.
Study instruction:
- "Interpret WCSS for a small dataset and explain why additional clusters can lower it without improving segmentation."
Key point:
- WCSS measures squared distances to centroids
- a lower value alone does not establish usefulness.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
- scikit-learn: K-Means
55. Q: How does the elbow method help select a K-means cluster count?
A: Show answer
The elbow method compares WCSS across candidate values of K and plots the results. Adding clusters usually reduces WCSS, but at some point the improvement may become smaller. The analyst looks for a bend where extra clusters deliver diminishing gains in compactness. This is an indication of a reasonable trade-off, not proof that the chosen number is uniquely correct. Some datasets show no clear elbow, and a visible bend may not correspond to meaningful business segments. Candidate solutions should also be checked for stability, cluster size, separation, and usefulness.
Study instruction:
- "Create a WCSS-versus-K plot, identify any elbow, and explain why cluster profiling remains necessary."
Key point:
- The elbow method looks for diminishing improvement as K increases
- it does not prove the true group count.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
- scikit-learn: K-Means
56. Q: What are the main limitations of K-means clustering?
A: Show answer
K-means requires a predefined cluster count and relies on distances to mean centroids. It can be sensitive to initial centroid positions and may converge to a local solution rather than the best possible one. It is most natural for clusters compact around their centers; it can perform poorly when groups have very different sizes, densities, or non-spherical shapes. Outliers can pull means away from the bulk of observations. Feature scaling and irrelevant variables can distort assignments. Analysts should check initialization, preparation, stability, and whether the data geometry fits a centroid-based method.
Study instruction:
- "Identify an outlier, a non-spherical cluster, and a scale mismatch and explain how each could distort K-means."
Key point:
- K-means is sensitive to initialization, scaling, and geometry
- compactness alone does not establish a meaningful segment.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
- scikit-learn: K-Means
57. Q: What is hierarchical clustering and how does it differ from K-means?
A: Show answer
Hierarchical clustering builds nested groups by repeatedly merging smaller clusters or splitting larger ones. The chapter focuses on agglomerative hierarchical clustering, a bottom-up approach that starts with each observation as its own cluster and joins groups according to a distance and linkage rule. Unlike standard K-means, it does not begin by requiring one fixed K for the entire process; the hierarchy can be examined at different cut levels. K-means maintains a specified number of centroid-based groups and iteratively reassigns observations. Hierarchical methods show relationships at several levels, but results depend on distance and linkage choices and may be costly for large datasets.
Study instruction:
- "Compare K-means with agglomerative clustering, explaining how each represents similarity and chooses a partition."
Key point:
- Agglomerative clustering builds groups through merges
- K-means uses a fixed K and centroids.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
58. Q: How does agglomerative hierarchical clustering work?
A: Show answer
Agglomerative clustering begins with each observation in its own cluster. It calculates which pair of clusters is closest under a chosen distance and linkage rule, merges that pair, and repeats with the remaining clusters. As the process continues, the number of clusters decreases and a nested hierarchy forms. The sequence can be represented as a dendrogram, with merge heights indicating the distance at which groups were joined. A user can choose a cut through that hierarchy to obtain a number of groups. Because early merges are normally not undone, distance, linkage, scaling, and outlier treatment can materially affect the result.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a six-point agglomerative example and show the sequence of merges until all points belong to one group."
Key point:
- Agglomerative clustering starts with individual observations and merges clusters
- the sequence creates a hierarchy.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
59. Q: What is the role of distance and linkage in hierarchical clustering?
A: Show answer
Distance describes dissimilarity between observations or cluster representatives, while linkage specifies how distance between multi-observation clusters is calculated. Different linkage rules can select different merge pairs from the same data. Single linkage focuses on the closest cross-cluster pair; complete linkage focuses on the farthest pair; average linkage uses the average of cross-cluster distances; Ward's method merges clusters to minimize the increase in within-cluster variance. These rules embody different notions of what it means for groups to be close. An analyst should select a rule that fits the data shape and interpretive purpose rather than treating linkage as an invisible default.
Study instruction:
- "For two small clusters, describe single, complete, and average linkage and explain why their rankings may differ."
Key point:
- Distance and linkage jointly determine which clusters merge
- linkage rules encode different definitions of proximity.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
60. Q: What are single, complete, and average linkage in hierarchical clustering?
A: Show answer
Single linkage defines cluster distance by the closest pair of observations across clusters. Complete linkage uses the farthest such pair, emphasizing maximum separation between cluster members. Average linkage uses the average distance across cross-cluster pairs. These definitions can yield different hierarchies: single linkage can connect groups through chains of nearby points, while complete linkage favors groups whose members remain relatively close even at their most separated boundary. Average linkage considers all cross-cluster pairs. No rule is universally best; analysts should consider desired cluster shape, sensitivity to noise, and the practical meaning of a merge.
Study instruction:
- "Use a distance matrix to calculate or describe single-link, complete-link, and average-link distances."
Key point:
- Linkage rules summarize cluster distance differently
- the chosen rule changes the hierarchy and needs justification.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
61. Q: What is a dendrogram and how can it help choose a cluster solution?
A: Show answer
A dendrogram is a tree diagram showing which observations or clusters are merged at each step and the distance associated with each merge. It shows nested relationships rather than only one fixed partition. A cut through the diagram yields a set of clusters at that level. Large jumps in merge distance can suggest previously distinct groups are being forced together, providing a possible cut location. The cut still requires judgment: a convenient level may produce clusters too small, imbalanced, unstable, or irrelevant to the task. Cluster profiles should be examined before adopting the result.
Study instruction:
- "Interpret a dendrogram by selecting a candidate cut, counting the resulting groups, and explaining the limitations."
Key point:
- A dendrogram visualizes merges and distances
- a cut creates a partition but does not validate usefulness.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
62. Q: How should an analyst select between K-means and hierarchical clustering?
A: Show answer
K-means is attractive when there is a reasonable candidate cluster count, data can be summarized by centroids, and an iterative method suits the dataset size. Hierarchical clustering helps when the analyst wants to explore nested relationships or inspect groupings at several levels. K-means can be efficient on larger datasets, while agglomerative methods may become costly as observations grow. Both depend on feature choice, scaling, distance definitions, and interpretation. Selection should reflect data geometry, scale, need for hierarchy, and intended use. Comparing methods can show whether broad conclusions are robust.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a method for customer segmentation and list the evidence that would change your choice."
Key point:
- The methods address related but different grouping tasks
- geometry, scale, interpretability, and use should drive selection.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
- scikit-learn: K-Means
63. Q: How can an analyst assess whether discovered clusters are meaningful rather than arbitrary labels?
A: Show answer
Profile each cluster using original features, check whether groups are distinct, assess sensitivity to changes in samples or settings, and ask whether the pattern is useful for the intended decision. A label such as Cluster 2 has no inherent meaning; it must be translated into a description reflecting observed members without exaggeration. Stability checks reveal whether a small data change produces a different segmentation. Domain experts may identify artifacts, hidden constraints, or missing variables. If clusters cannot be explained or do not support useful action, reconsider features, settings, or whether clustering was appropriate.
Study instruction:
- "Create a cluster profile covering size, feature distributions, distinguishing attributes, stability, and cautious interpretation."
Key point:
- Cluster meaning comes from its members and use case
- stability and utility matter alongside compactness.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
64. Q: Why can feature scaling and feature selection affect clustering so strongly?
A: Show answer
Many clustering methods use distances, so features with larger numeric ranges can dominate similarity. A segmentation using income and a score on a small scale may mostly reflect income unless variables are scaled or deliberately weighted. Irrelevant variables add dimensions that can obscure structure, while duplicated or correlated measures can unintentionally give one concept extra weight. Feature selection should reflect the question the clusters are meant to answer, and scaling should be applied consistently. These steps do not guarantee meaningful groups, but make the geometry more consistent with the intended notion of similarity. Document the choices so results can be reproduced and interpreted.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect units and distributions of candidate features, then propose a justified scaling and feature-selection plan."
Key point:
- Distance-based clusters depend on variables and their scales
- feature choices define similarity.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Clustering
- scikit-learn: K-Means
65. Q: How can classification results be evaluated without testing on training data?
A: Show answer
A model should be assessed using observations not used to fit it. If the same records are used for training and assessment, a model can appear excellent because it has already seen their labels; this does not show how it will behave on new cases. A common design separates training and test sets, with cross-validation or a validation set used to compare settings. The final test set should remain separate until model and threshold choices are settled. For imbalanced classes, overall accuracy may conceal poor detection of the less common class, so class-specific errors and decision consequences should also be examined.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a data-splitting plan identifying fitting, tuning, and final evaluation data, and explain why the test set must remain untouched."
Key point:
- Evaluate generalization on data not used to train or tune
- repeated test-set tuning leaks information.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Cross-validation
66. Q: What is overfitting and how can it affect methods in this chapter?
A: Show answer
Overfitting occurs when a model captures patterns or noise specific to training data and performs less well on unseen cases. A deep tree can create narrow rules around unusual observations; K-NN with very small k can be overly sensitive to local noise; a repeatedly tuned model may fit quirks in the validation set. Clustering can produce very fine groupings that look neat within the current sample but are unstable elsewhere. Separate evaluation data, cross-validation, complexity controls, sensible features, and stability checks help identify overfitting. The objective is dependable conclusions for new observations, not a perfect description of the training sample.
Study instruction:
- "For a deep tree, small-k K-NN, and fine-grained clustering result, identify an overfitting symptom and a test."
Key point:
- Overfitting is a gap between fit on seen data and performance on new cases
- validation and stability checks assess generalization.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Cross-validation
- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
- scikit-learn: Nearest Neighbors
67. Q: What should an analyst do when predictions or cluster assignments conflict with domain knowledge?
A: Show answer
Treat the conflict as a diagnostic signal rather than automatically accepting or rejecting the algorithm. First check source records, labels, feature definitions, date cut-offs, missing values, scaling, and transformations. Then determine whether the domain expectation is supported by evidence or is itself an assumption needing review. Inspect tree paths, nearest neighbours, probabilities, or cluster profiles to understand how the output arose. If it is a data error, correct the governed source and retrain; if it reflects an omitted factor or changed environment, improve the model representation. Record the issue, its resolution, and remaining uncertainty before using the result.
Study instruction:
- "Write an investigation checklist separating data-quality checks, model-behavior checks, domain assumptions, and escalation."
Key point:
- Unexpected outputs should trigger investigation
- domain knowledge is a review control, not a substitute for evidence.
Source links:
- scikit-learn: Decision Trees
- scikit-learn: Cross-validation
- scikit-learn: Clustering
68. Q: How should a practitioner communicate classification or clustering results to non-technical stakeholders?
A: Show answer
Start with the business question and state what the method actually produces: a predicted class, probability, or similarity-based groups. Explain main inputs, assumptions, limitations, and evaluation evidence. For classification, distinguish probability from the threshold-based label and describe false-positive and false-negative consequences. For clustering, describe group profiles rather than relying on arbitrary cluster numbers. Communication should identify what decisions the analysis can support and what it cannot establish, especially regarding causation or high-impact actions. A plain-language summary should enable stakeholders to question the logic and use findings responsibly.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a non-technical brief with purpose, data, method, results, limitations, and decision implications."
Key point:
- Explain outputs and assumptions in decision-relevant language
- communicate uncertainty and limitations.

69. Q: What practical lessons should a learner retain from Chapter 6 about choosing and using algorithms?
A: Show answer
The practical lesson is to start with the analytical question and choose a method that matches the task. Use classification when reliable target categories are known and clustering when the goal is to explore unknown group structure. Understand algorithm intuition, but do not mistake an accessible explanation for proof that assumptions are satisfied. Trees produce sequential rules; random forests aggregate trees; K-NN relies on local distance; Bayes methods reason from probabilities; logistic regression estimates class probabilities; K-means forms centroid-based groups; hierarchical clustering builds nested merges. Good practice also requires relevant features, sound preparation, careful evaluation, and critical interpretation. No algorithm substitutes for a clear question or professional judgment.
