Rows: 71
Table 9
Data Presentation: Storytelling, Stakeholder Communication, and Executive Presentations
1. Q: What is the central purpose of Chapter 9, “Data Presentation”?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains how a data scientist should communicate the results of a project to the people who commissioned it and need to act on the findings. Its central argument is that analytical skill alone is not enough: insight creates value only when stakeholders can understand what was learned, why it matters and what should happen next. The presentation stage converts the work performed across the Data Science Process into a coherent message suited to an audience that may not know data science, statistics or the technical systems involved. Poor communication can cause decision-makers to ignore a valid result or act on a mistaken interpretation. The chapter therefore treats storytelling, audience awareness, plain language, presentation structure, rehearsal and effective answers to questions as essential professional capabilities.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter in terms of audience, message, evidence and action; identify how a technically correct result could still fail through poor communication."
Key point:
- Data science is not complete when the analysis is complete; stakeholders must understand and use the findings.
- The ability to communicate insight is a core professional skill, not an optional finishing touch.
Source links:
- United Nations Statistics Division: Data Storytelling
2. Q: Why is communication the final stage of the Data Science Process rather than an administrative afterthought?
A: Show answer
The final stage is where analytical work reaches the people who initiated the project and can decide whether to implement a recommendation. Earlier stages identify a question, prepare data, analyse it and visualize results; presentation brings those elements together for a decision. If the intended users cannot understand the question, the method, the finding or its implications, the analysis may not change the process it was designed to improve. Communication also exposes unanswered questions: when the analyst tries to explain a result, gaps in assumptions, limitations or evidence may become visible. The presentation stage is therefore both a delivery activity and a final opportunity to check whether the project has produced a clear, supportable and actionable answer.
Study instruction:
- "For a completed analysis, map each project stage to one sentence in the final presentation and show how the final recommendation follows from the earlier evidence."
Key point:
- The presentation stage connects technical work to its intended users and decisions.
- Preparing the message can reveal unresolved analytical questions before the project closes.
Source links:
- United Nations Statistics Division: Data Storytelling
3. Q: What does storytelling mean in a data science presentation?
A: Show answer
Storytelling means organizing the project's question, investigative journey, evidence and conclusion into a narrative that an audience can follow. It is not permission to invent drama, conceal inconvenient results or make the data fit a predetermined storyline. The analyst should explain why the project began, what needed to be understood, how important problems were addressed, what the results show and what remains unanswered. A story provides order and context so that stakeholders can understand the relationship between decisions made during the project and the outcome. It should keep the data at the centre while making the human and operational significance clear. A good narrative is specific to the actual project rather than a generic sequence of impressive charts.
Study instruction:
- "Write a five-sentence narrative covering the original problem, investigation, turning point, evidence-based finding and decision required."
Key point:
- A data story makes the path from question to evidence and action understandable.
- Narrative structure must clarify the facts, not distort or selectively present them.
Source links:
- United Nations Statistics Division: Data Storytelling
- Innovations for Poverty Action: Communicating with Data
4. Q: How can the start-to-finish structure described in the chapter help organize a presentation?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends writing down the project's starting point, A, and its intended or achieved endpoint, B, then filling the space between them with the milestones that explain how the team moved from one to the other. The milestones can follow the Data Science Process: defining the question, preparing the information, analysing it, visualizing the results and presenting the conclusion. The analyst should identify the decisions, obstacles, discoveries and surprises that materially changed the direction of the work. This approach creates a logical structure without requiring a complicated presentation formula. The audience can see where the work began, what happened along the way and how the evidence supports the endpoint. It is also a useful test of whether the project has a coherent explanation.
Study instruction:
- "Create an A-to-B outline for a real project and identify the three to five milestones that are necessary to understand the result."
Key point:
- A clear presentation takes the audience from the initial problem to the evidence-supported outcome.
- Milestones should explain the project's reasoning, not reproduce every activity in chronological detail.
Source links:
- United Nations Statistics Division: Data Storytelling
5. Q: How can a presenter use the Data Science Process as the narrative structure?
A: Show answer
The Data Science Process gives the presentation a familiar logical sequence. Begin with the question that needed to be answered, explain what data was required and how its quality was assessed, describe the analytical approach in language suited to the audience, show the relevant visualization and finish with the implications and proposed action. The presenter should not explain every technical step with equal weight. Instead, select the stages that explain how the conclusion was reached or why a particular decision was necessary. A stakeholder should understand the chain of reasoning well enough to distinguish evidence from assumptions and to ask informed questions. Where the actual project departed from the planned process, explain the reason and its effect on the result.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a slide outline using the five stages and mark which technical details are essential for the audience to trust the conclusion."
Key point:
- The process sequence helps the audience understand how the result was produced.
- Explain the steps that matter to the decision, while retaining technical detail for questions or an appendix.
Source links:
- United Nations Statistics Division: Data Storytelling
6. Q: Why should a data scientist identify unanswered questions before presenting?
A: Show answer
A presentation should not imply that every aspect of a project has been resolved when important uncertainties remain. The chapter recommends revisiting the stages of the project and asking what questions needed answering, how problems were handled, what the results show and whether anything remains unanswered. This review helps the presenter distinguish a completed finding from a provisional interpretation or a recommendation that needs further testing. It also prepares the presenter for questions from managers who may notice a missing data source, unusual observation or operational consequence. Explicitly identifying a limitation can strengthen credibility because it shows that the analyst understands the boundary of the evidence and is not overstating the result.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a section in your speaker notes listing confirmed findings, assumptions, unresolved issues and the additional evidence needed to resolve them."
Key point:
- A credible presentation makes material gaps and limitations visible.
- Unanswered questions should be identified, explained and assigned a sensible next step.
Source links:
- CDC: Clear Communication Index FAQs
7. Q: Who is the end user of a data science project, and why should the presentation focus on that person?
A: Show answer
The end user is the person or group that will interpret the findings and use them to make a decision or change a process. It may be an executive, operational manager, customer-facing team, policy-maker, programme manager or another stakeholder who initiated the project. The chapter emphasizes that the end user may have limited technical knowledge and may be unfamiliar with the methods used. Focusing on that person means explaining the result in terms of their responsibilities, constraints and decisions, not simply describing the analysis from the analyst's perspective. The presenter should ask what the audience needs to know, what it already understands and what action it can realistically take. That does not mean hiding complexity; it means making complexity accessible.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the decision-maker and operational users for a project and write down the question each group needs answered."
Key point:
- A presentation should be designed around the people who must understand or act on the findings.
- Audience focus changes the explanation, examples and requested action without changing the underlying evidence.
Source links:
- United Nations Statistics Division: Data Storytelling
- CDC: Developing Effective Communication Products
8. Q: How should a presenter adapt a technical explanation for an audience with limited data science knowledge?
A: Show answer
Start with the practical problem and use ordinary language to describe the method's purpose. Explain a technical term the first time it appears, avoid unexplained acronyms and connect the method to a familiar process or example. Instead of listing algorithms, explain what information the model uses, what it predicts, how the result is evaluated and how a person should use it. Keep the logic accurate: simplifying the vocabulary must not remove an important qualification or make the model sound more certain than it is. The chapter recommends making complex ideas simple and appealing to the person with the least background in the room when the audience's knowledge is uncertain. The presenter can provide technical detail separately for specialists who need it.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a technical paragraph for a non-specialist and have a colleague explain back what the method does, what it does not do and why it matters."
Key point:
- Use plain language, define necessary terms and connect technical methods to practical outcomes.
- Simplification should improve access without changing the meaning or limitations of the analysis.
Source links:
- CDC: Clear Communication Index FAQs
- Innovations for Poverty Action: Communicating with Data
9. Q: What does the Veros Credit case study show about communicating a complex predictive model?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a vehicle-finance company whose data science team built a credit-scoring model to estimate the risk associated with individual loan applicants. The model used customer attributes such as credit score and employment history, and its output informed the lender's decision and the terms offered to dealerships. The model was monitored monthly and adjusted as additional data became available. The case study's communication lesson is that the most difficult part for the senior risk manager was obtaining managerial and executive approval, not merely developing the model. He therefore explained the project in language suited to the end users and decision-makers, drawing on experience teaching statistics to non-specialists. Technical sophistication did not remove the need to explain why the model was useful and how it worked.
Study instruction:
- "For a predictive model, prepare a non-technical explanation of its purpose, input categories, output, monitoring process and decision role."
Key point:
- A technically strong model still needs an understandable business case and clear explanation.
- The explanation should connect model output to the decisions made by its intended users.
Source links:
- CDC: Developing Effective Communication Products
10. Q: How should a presenter explain a predictive model to executives without oversimplifying it?
A: Show answer
Explain the decision the model supports, the data categories it uses, the type of output it produces, the way its performance is evaluated and the controls around its use. Use a simple example to illustrate the process, but distinguish the example from a guarantee about every case. Executives should understand the model's intended population, key limitations, monitoring frequency, human responsibilities and consequences of an incorrect prediction. Avoid presenting a score as an objective truth or describing a model as eliminating risk. If the audience needs technical validation detail, provide a concise explanation of the evaluation approach and make supporting documentation available. A clear explanation helps management challenge the assumptions and approve an appropriate use rather than accept a black box.
Study instruction:
- "Build a one-page model briefing that answers: what decision, for whom, based on what data, with what performance evidence, limitations and oversight?"
Key point:
- Executives need the model's purpose, decision role, performance and safeguards in understandable terms.
- Do not turn a prediction into a guarantee or remove essential limitations.
Source links:
- United Nations Statistics Division: Data Storytelling
- CDC: Clear Communication Index FAQs
11. Q: Why might an executive interpret an outlier as evidence that a project has failed, and how should the analyst respond?
A: Show answer
A stakeholder who is unfamiliar with statistical variation may assume that any unusual observation invalidates the model or project. The chapter uses this concern to explain why data scientists must anticipate how non-specialists may read results. The analyst should define what an outlier means in the relevant context, explain how it was identified, show whether it is an isolated observation or part of a wider pattern and describe how it affects the model or recommendation. The presenter should not dismiss the concern or hide the observation. Instead, explain whether the record is a data error, a legitimate exceptional case or evidence that the model requires further investigation. If the issue affects the decision, state the risk and the proposed response clearly.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare an example outlier and a plain-language explanation of its impact, possible causes and the checks needed before drawing a conclusion."
Key point:
- An outlier is a signal to understand, not automatic proof of project failure.
- Explain how unusual observations affect the result and what investigation or safeguard is appropriate.
Source links:
- CDC: Clear Communication Index FAQs
12. Q: What is data advocacy, and how can a data scientist create it within an organization?
A: Show answer
Data advocacy is the process of helping colleagues recognize the value of using data to understand problems and improve decisions. The chapter argues that data science is not equally familiar to every department, so practitioners should explain its practical relevance rather than assume that others already understand the discipline. A clear presentation can turn a stakeholder into an advocate who shares the result and encourages other teams to consider data-driven approaches. Advocacy is not simply promoting tools or persuading people to accept every analysis. It requires demonstrating value with credible evidence, explaining limitations and showing how the work helps the stakeholder's own responsibilities. Follow-up and collaboration help make the initial success useful beyond the original project.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a neighbouring department that could benefit from a completed analysis and prepare a short explanation of a relevant use case and its limitations."
Key point:
- Data advocacy grows when people see credible benefits in their own work.
- The goal is informed adoption of data-driven problem-solving, not uncritical promotion of analytics.
Source links:
- United Nations Statistics Division: Data Storytelling
13. Q: What does the Sunsuper communications-processing case study teach about presenting results beyond the immediate deliverable?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a project to reduce a backlog of customer-service communications associated with an annual campaign. The analyst used data science to reduce the reported backlog from 40 days to three days. Instead of stopping when the result was achieved, he offered to present the approach, findings and results to the operations department. That presentation helped colleagues understand what had changed and why the method was useful, leading them to ask further questions about improving efficiency. The case study illustrates that a result can have broader value when the analyst explains how it was achieved and helps the people who run the process learn from it. It does not mean that the improvement should be attributed to data science without examining the operational changes and evidence behind the result.
Study instruction:
- "For a completed improvement project, record the baseline, intervention, outcome, period, measurement method and the operational practices others could reuse."
Key point:
- The chapter's example reports a reduction in backlog from 40 days to three days.
- Sharing the method and learning can extend a project's value beyond the initial result.
Source links:
- United Nations Statistics Division: Data Storytelling
14. Q: Why should a data scientist present findings even when a formal presentation was not requested?
A: Show answer
A short, appropriate presentation can ensure that the people affected by a project understand its outcome and the work that produced it. The chapter recommends taking initiative when the result may help other teams learn about data science or identify further improvements. Without an explanation, stakeholders may see only a number or an operational change without understanding its causes, limits or how it could be repeated. However, initiative should remain proportionate and respectful of the organization's priorities, confidentiality and communication channels. The presenter should agree on the audience and purpose, avoid duplicating an established reporting process and focus on useful learning rather than self-promotion. When the work is sensitive, obtain the necessary authorization before sharing it.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one project result that could help another team and define a short, useful knowledge-sharing session with an appropriate audience and permission."
Key point:
- Sharing results can help stakeholders understand and reuse successful practices.
- Initiative should serve organizational learning while respecting confidentiality and reporting requirements.
Source links:
- United Nations Statistics Division: Data Storytelling
15. Q: How can the presenter develop a strong opening by stating the problem upfront?
A: Show answer
The opening should remind the audience of the problem that the project was commissioned to solve. The chapter recommends keeping this statement as concise as possible, ideally one sentence when the problem can genuinely be expressed that way. A clear problem statement gives the audience a reference point for the evidence and recommendations that follow. It should identify the affected process, population or outcome without introducing technical details prematurely. A vague opening such as 'we analysed a lot of data' tells the audience what the analyst did, not why the work matters. The problem statement should be accurate, agreed with relevant stakeholders and narrow enough that the final conclusion can answer it.
Study instruction:
- "Write a one-sentence problem statement and test whether a colleague can identify the affected process, the issue and why it matters."
Key point:
- State the business or operational problem before explaining the method.
- A concise opening gives the audience a clear frame for the rest of the presentation.
Source links:
- United Nations Statistics Division: Data Storytelling
- CDC: Developing Effective Communication Products
16. Q: Why should a presenter explain the benefit of the analysis early in the presentation?
A: Show answer
The audience needs to know why its attention is valuable. After stating the problem, the presenter should preview the benefit that the evidence may support, such as shorter processing time, improved customer experience, better targeting or reduced operational risk. The benefit should be tied to the actual results and not presented as a promise before it has been demonstrated. Early relevance helps stakeholders understand why they should listen, but the rest of the presentation must substantiate the claim with the baseline, analytical method, outcome and limitations. If the result is uncertain or requires a decision to realize the benefit, say so. The presenter should distinguish an observed improvement from an anticipated future gain.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a two-sentence opening: first the problem, then the evidenced or expected benefit; label any forecast as an estimate rather than a realized result."
Key point:
- Show why the presentation matters early, after defining the problem.
- Distinguish measured outcomes from projected benefits and proposed improvements.
Source links:
- Innovations for Poverty Action: Communicating with Data
17. Q: How should a data scientist quantify the financial value of a project for executives?
A: Show answer
The chapter advises considering the financial consequences of a proposed organizational or strategic change, even when the main finding concerns customer experience or efficiency. Quantification may involve expected revenue retained, additional income, processing costs avoided, implementation costs, ongoing operating costs or the value of time released for other work. The analyst should work with finance and relevant operational teams to validate the assumptions and use an appropriate time horizon. A calculation should distinguish actual savings from cost avoidance, projected benefits and non-financial outcomes. It should also disclose uncertainty, implementation dependencies and any costs that are excluded. A monetary estimate is useful when it informs a decision; it becomes misleading when assumptions are hidden or an illustrative calculation is presented as a guaranteed return.
Study instruction:
- "Construct a simple benefit-and-cost model with baseline, volume, unit cost, implementation cost, time period and scenario assumptions; obtain review from the responsible finance team."
Key point:
- Financial translation can help executives compare the project's costs and expected benefits.
- Assumptions, uncertainty, timing and the distinction between realized and forecast benefits must be explicit.
Source links:
- CDC: Clear Communication Index FAQs
18. Q: How can an analyst communicate non-financial benefits when a project's value is not captured by revenue?
A: Show answer
Some projects improve service quality, reduce waiting time, strengthen compliance, lower error rates, increase accessibility or help customers receive a better experience. These outcomes can still be measured and explained even when they do not translate directly into revenue. The presenter should identify the relevant baseline, define the outcome metric, show the change and explain who benefits. Where a financial proxy is used, disclose the assumptions and avoid presenting it as a cash saving unless it is actually realized. Non-financial benefits may be central to the organization's purpose or obligations, so they should not be treated as secondary merely because they are harder to price. A balanced presentation can show both operational outcomes and financial consequences.
Study instruction:
- "Select one non-financial project outcome and define its measure, baseline, affected population and evidence of improvement."
Key point:
- Project value includes service, quality, risk and social outcomes as well as financial results.
- Measure non-financial benefits directly where possible rather than forcing every result into a monetary estimate.
Source links:
- United Nations Statistics Division: Data Storytelling
19. Q: Why should a presenter prepare for hard questions from executives and managers?
A: Show answer
Executives are accountable for organizational performance and may be cautious about a new method, an unusual result or an investment that introduces risk. A knowledge gap between the analyst and the audience does not remove the audience's responsibility to challenge the proposal. The chapter recommends anticipating difficult questions and responding with facts rather than becoming defensive. Likely questions concern data quality, model performance, uncertainty, costs, implementation, risks, alternatives and accountability. Preparing answers helps the analyst recognize where the evidence is strong and where additional analysis is required. A presenter should be willing to say that an answer is not yet known, explain what would establish it and avoid inventing a confident response under pressure.
Study instruction:
- "Brainstorm ten difficult questions about the project and prepare evidence-based answers, including at least two questions whose honest answer is that more work is needed."
Key point:
- Executive scrutiny is part of responsible decision-making, not an attack on the analyst.
- Prepare facts and limitations; do not respond defensively or fabricate certainty.
Source links:
- CDC: Clear Communication Index FAQs
20. Q: How should a presenter prepare for the question-and-answer session?
A: Show answer
The presenter should anticipate the questions likely to arise from the audience's responsibilities and concerns. Prepare to explain unusual findings, gaps, model behaviour, cost assumptions, implementation requirements and the consequences of acting or not acting. The chapter suggests considering how a known problem or anomaly can be introduced in the presentation so that it is not a surprise during the discussion. The presenter should not manipulate the audience or hide an issue to engineer a question; the purpose is to explain the issue transparently and be prepared to address it. Keep supporting evidence available, identify which questions can be answered from the current analysis and which require follow-up, and record commitments made during the session.
Study instruction:
- "Create a Q&A preparation sheet with likely question, concise answer, supporting evidence, limitation and follow-up owner."
Key point:
- Prepare for questions about the risks, gaps and implications that decision-makers are likely to notice.
- Address known issues transparently and track questions that require further analysis.
Source links:
- CDC: Developing Effective Communication Products
21. Q: How should a presenter respond when a question cannot be answered from the available evidence?
A: Show answer
Acknowledge the question, state what the current data or analysis can establish and explain what remains unknown. Do not guess or present an assumption as a fact to avoid an awkward pause. If the question is material, identify the additional data, validation or expertise required, agree on who will investigate it and provide a realistic follow-up route. If the question falls outside the project's agreed scope, explain that boundary and decide whether a separate analysis is appropriate. This response preserves credibility and gives the audience a clear understanding of the limits of the recommendation. It also prevents a tentative answer from being repeated later as an approved finding.
Study instruction:
- "Practice responding to an unanswerable but relevant question in three parts: current evidence, unresolved issue and next verification step."
Key point:
- An honest evidence boundary is more credible than an unsupported answer.
- Turn material unanswered questions into explicit follow-up actions.
Source links:
- CDC: Clear Communication Index FAQs
22. Q: How can a presenter anticipate and explain anomalies without allowing them to dominate the discussion?
A: Show answer
First establish whether the anomaly is real, how it was identified and whether it materially changes the conclusion. If it is important, explain it in the presentation with sufficient context so that the audience does not discover it without guidance in the Q&A. Describe possible causes, the tests performed and any remaining uncertainty. If the anomaly does not change the central finding, explain why and retain the supporting evidence. If it does change the finding, adjust the recommendation rather than attempting to minimize the issue. The chapter's practical advice is to prepare for predictable questions, but transparency is essential: the aim is to help the audience understand the issue, not to distract it from unfavourable evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Choose the most material anomaly in a project and prepare an explanation showing its size, cause if known, impact and next action."
Key point:
- A material anomaly should be explained with evidence and context.
- Do not conceal a weakness or imply that an unresolved exception has been settled.
Source links:
- CDC: Clear Communication Index FAQs
23. Q: How can a presenter engage an audience that appears tired, distracted or sceptical?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes interaction as one way to regain attention, including a relevant opening question or a light, appropriate moment of humour. A show-of-hands question can reveal how familiar the audience is with a topic and give listeners a reason to participate. Humour should fit the presenter, subject and organizational setting; a forced joke or humour directed at a stakeholder can undermine trust. For a sceptical audience, a useful question may invite people to consider the operational problem rather than pressure them to agree with the recommendation. Engagement techniques should support the message and make the room more receptive, not manipulate the audience or take time away from essential evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare one relevant question that invites the audience to reflect on the project problem and decide whether it is suitable for the meeting."
Key point:
- Interaction can create attention and give the audience a role in the discussion.
- Use humour cautiously and never let an engagement technique replace evidence.
Source links:
- CDC: Developing Effective Communication Products
24. Q: How should the presenter decide whether to use humour or a question as an opening interaction?
A: Show answer
The choice should reflect the presenter's natural communication style, the audience's expectations and the seriousness of the subject. The chapter suggests that a light self-directed joke may work in some larger settings, while a relevant question can be effective in a boardroom or smaller meeting. The presenter should not assume that one technique suits every culture, hierarchy or situation. In a sensitive discussion about risk, customer harm or a difficult operational decision, a direct question may be more appropriate than humour. The purpose is to establish attention and participation without diminishing the importance of the issue. A simple, respectful interaction is usually safer than a rehearsed joke that distracts from the project's message.
Study instruction:
- "Compare two possible openings for the same presentation and assess which better fits the audience, setting, subject sensitivity and time available."
Key point:
- Select an engagement technique that is natural and appropriate to the room.
- Respectful relevance is more important than entertaining the audience.
Source links:
- CDC: Developing Effective Communication Products
25. Q: Why is jargon-free language especially important in data science presentations?
A: Show answer
A mixed audience may include people with different levels of technical knowledge and different responsibilities. Unexplained language can cause some listeners to misunderstand the method, while others may stop following the argument entirely. The chapter recommends appealing to the least technically experienced person when the presenter's knowledge of the audience is uncertain. This means using short, direct sentences, defining unavoidable terms and explaining why a method matters to the problem. Technical words should be used when they are precise and necessary, not as a display of expertise. A jargon-free explanation still needs to be accurate: the presenter should not replace a precise concept with a vague analogy that changes its meaning.
Study instruction:
- "List the technical terms in a project presentation and decide which can be removed, which need a definition and which must remain for precision."
Key point:
- Clarity requires language the whole intended audience can follow.
- Plain language is not the same as imprecise or oversimplified reasoning.
Source links:
- CDC: Clear Communication Index FAQs
- Innovations for Poverty Action: Communicating with Data
26. Q: How can a presenter explain a technical method without merely listing tools and algorithms?
A: Show answer
Describe the practical task the method performs and why that task is relevant. For example, instead of naming several model types, explain that the analysis estimates which applications are more likely to result in a specified outcome, what evidence is used and how performance is checked. If an algorithm name is relevant to a technical audience, define it briefly and explain its role. The presenter should connect the method to the decision and distinguish what the system calculates from what a human must decide. This allows stakeholders to evaluate whether the method fits their problem without requiring them to become specialists in every technique. A short walkthrough of the project can show how the method was applied in practice.
Study instruction:
- "Replace a tool-focused slide with a process diagram that shows input, analytical step, output, validation and human decision."
Key point:
- Explain what a method does for the project before naming its technical components.
- Keep the distinction between automated output and human responsibility clear.
Source links:
- United Nations Statistics Division: Data Storytelling
27. Q: Why should a presenter tell the audience how the findings relate to its own department or work?
A: Show answer
A result becomes more relevant when stakeholders can see how it affects a process, customer group or decision for which they are responsible. The chapter encourages the analyst to learn about the departments represented in the audience and to explain practical applications rather than promoting data science only as a theoretical discipline. A finance manager may need to understand control implications, an operations manager may need to see processing consequences and an executive may need to assess investment or risk. The central evidence remains the same, but examples and implications should connect to the audience's work. This approach helps people see whether the method can be reused and what support would be required.
Study instruction:
- "For three stakeholder groups, write one practical implication from the same finding and note what decision or workflow each group controls."
Key point:
- Audience relevance grows when findings are connected to real responsibilities and processes.
- Adapt examples and implications without altering the facts or conclusion.
Source links:
- United Nations Statistics Division: Data Storytelling
28. Q: How can a personal or human story strengthen a presentation about analytical work?
A: Show answer
A human story can help listeners understand why the problem matters and remember how the analyst worked through it. The chapter recommends reflecting on surprises, interactions, setbacks, personal experience and the practical effect of the result. A short example of a customer waiting for a service, a team struggling with a backlog or a decision-maker facing uncertainty can make an abstract data issue concrete. The story must be relevant and truthful, protect privacy and avoid using one person's experience as proof of a general pattern. It should illuminate the evidence rather than replace it. The analyst can combine a human example with the broader data so that the audience understands both the individual experience and the population-level finding.
Study instruction:
- "Find a privacy-safe example that illustrates the project's problem and pair it with the aggregate evidence; explain where the example is illustrative rather than representative."
Key point:
- Human context can make a data finding meaningful and memorable.
- Anecdotes illustrate; representative evidence is still needed for general conclusions.
Source links:
- United Nations Statistics Division: Data Storytelling
29. Q: Why should a data story include setbacks and unexpected discoveries rather than only positive results?
A: Show answer
A project narrative that includes relevant obstacles can show how the analyst investigated uncertainty, adapted the method and reached a defensible conclusion. The chapter argues that audiences can relate to the process of encountering and overcoming roadblocks, not just to a perfect success story. Explaining a setback may also help stakeholders understand the limits of the dataset, the choices made during preparation or the reason a recommendation changed. However, setbacks should be included because they explain the reasoning or outcome, not to manufacture drama. The presenter should avoid blaming individuals, revealing confidential details or exaggerating difficulty. A balanced story can show both what worked and what remains to be improved.
Study instruction:
- "Select one material project setback and explain its cause, response, learning and effect on the final result in a factual paragraph."
Key point:
- Relevant setbacks can make the project journey understandable and demonstrate learning.
- Include difficulties honestly and only where they help explain the evidence or decisions.
Source links:
- United Nations Statistics Division: Data Storytelling
30. Q: How should a presenter gather the materials needed to support the narrative?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends reviewing the visualizations already produced during the project and selecting the ones that best explain its journey and results. Depending on the topic, additional material may include process diagrams, flowcharts, product images, software screenshots, short video or audio elements and other contextual evidence. Every item should have a clear role in explaining the question, method, finding or recommendation. A logo or image used only for decoration does not strengthen the argument. The presenter should verify permissions, accuracy, currency and confidentiality, and should not rely on a screenshot when the underlying figure has changed. Materials should be organized so that the presenter can find supporting detail quickly during questions.
Study instruction:
- "Create a material list with the purpose, source, owner, date and permission status of each chart, image, diagram or media item."
Key point:
- Select presentation materials because they explain a milestone or support a conclusion.
- Verify accuracy, permissions and confidentiality before using external or internal media.
Source links:
- Microsoft Support: Tips for Creating and Delivering an Effective Presentation
31. Q: When are diagrams and flowcharts more useful than a chart of numerical results?
A: Show answer
A process diagram or flowchart is useful when the audience needs to understand sequence, roles, dependencies or how information moves through a system. A numerical chart is better suited to comparing values, distributions or trends. The chapter recommends using diagrams to explain concepts that may otherwise be difficult for a non-specialist to follow. For example, a flowchart can show how customer data enters a scoring process, how the output is checked and which person makes the final decision. The diagram should use clear labels, consistent symbols and a manageable level of detail. It should not imply that a workflow is automatic or mandatory when real decisions or exceptions remain outside the illustrated path.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one technical process and produce a simple flowchart that identifies input, steps, decisions, responsible roles and exceptions."
Key point:
- Use process diagrams to explain sequence and relationships that a numerical chart cannot show.
- Show human decisions and exception paths rather than presenting a simplified process as fully automated.

- Microsoft Support: Tips for Creating and Delivering an Effective Presentation
32. Q: How should an executive audience be approached differently from a technical or operational audience?
A: Show answer
Executives often need a concise explanation of the problem, the implications for the organization, the expected value, material risks and the decision required. They may have limited time and varying technical knowledge, so the presenter should organize the message clearly and avoid unnecessary detail. A technical audience may need more information about methods, data quality, validation and assumptions; an operational audience may need workflow steps, roles and implementation requirements. These differences affect emphasis, not factual consistency. The presenter should not hide a limitation from executives or provide a different conclusion to each group. A core evidence base should underpin all versions, while the examples, level of detail and requested action are adapted to the audience.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare executive, technical and operational versions of the same project summary and compare the details each audience needs to make its decision."
Key point:
- Adapt depth and emphasis to the audience's responsibilities and time constraints.
- Keep the evidence and conclusion consistent across every audience version.
