37. Q: How should a candidate prepare for a data-science interview?
A: Show answer
The chapter advises candidates to understand the industry and the employer, not only memorize algorithms and software names. Interviews may include technical discussion with data-science staff and broader business questions from managers. A candidate should be ready to describe projects, explain how methods were chosen, discuss limitations and show enthusiasm for the organization's work. They should also identify the business pain points where their skills could help. Preparation should be specific to the advertised role and include questions that test whether the job is a good fit for the candidate as well.
Study instruction:
- Prepare one technical project explanation and one business-focused explanation of the same work.
Key point:
- Interview readiness combines technical preparation with domain and business understanding.
- A strong candidate can explain both how they work and why their work matters to the employer.

38. Q: Why should interview preparation include technical and non-technical questions?
A: Show answer
A data-science interview may involve a technical practitioner assessing methods and a manager assessing communication, judgement, domain understanding or teamwork. Preparing only to recall code or formulas leaves the candidate unable to explain why an approach fits the business problem. Conversely, business fluency does not replace technical depth where the role requires it. The candidate should practise explaining a project at multiple levels, from the model or analytical design to the impact on a customer, process or decision.
Study instruction:
- For one project, prepare a five-minute technical explanation and a one-minute executive summary.
Key point:
- Different interviewers assess different parts of the role.
- Candidates should be able to move between methodological detail and practical meaning.
Source links:
- O*NET OnLine: Data Scientists

39. Q: What is a Fermi question in a data-science interview?
A: Show answer
A Fermi question asks for an estimate of a quantity that is not readily known, such as how many red cars are currently being driven in a country. It is designed to assess how a candidate breaks down an uncertain problem, chooses assumptions, uses approximate quantities and communicates a reasoned estimate. The chapter stresses that the interviewer is usually more interested in the logic than in an exact answer. A good response should make assumptions explicit, calculate transparently and acknowledge the estimate's uncertainty.
Study instruction:
- Practise estimating the number of bicycles in a city by defining the population, ownership rate and assumptions before calculating.
Key point:
- Fermi questions test structured estimation and reasoning under uncertainty.
- The clarity of assumptions and calculation matters more than false precision.
Source links:
- O*NET OnLine: Data Scientists
40. Q: What is a good step-by-step method for answering a Fermi question?
A: Show answer
Start by clarifying what is being counted and the geographic or time boundary. Break the problem into quantities that can be estimated, state assumptions for each quantity, calculate in a transparent sequence and sanity-check the result using an alternative approach or reasonable range. The chapter's red-car example estimates families, cars per family and the proportion of cars that are red. Its specific assumptions are illustrative rather than reliable current facts. A strong candidate should communicate uncertainty, avoid pretending to have exact data and revise an assumption if the resulting estimate seems implausible.
Study instruction:
- Answer a Fermi question using a numbered chain of assumptions, a calculation and a final plausibility check.
Key point:
- Decomposition makes an unfamiliar estimation problem manageable.
- An explicit range and sensitivity check are often more credible than a single overconfident number.
Source links:
- O*NET OnLine: Data Scientists
41. Q: How should a candidate communicate assumptions in a Fermi estimate?
A: Show answer
Assumptions should be stated before or alongside the calculation so the interviewer can understand how the estimate was derived. The candidate should explain why each assumption is plausible, identify which assumptions have the greatest effect on the answer and revise them if challenged. If a real statistic is not available, it is better to say so and use a clearly labelled approximation than to invent an authoritative figure. A range can represent uncertainty, while sensitivity analysis shows how the answer changes when one assumption changes.
Study instruction:
- Take a Fermi estimate and vary its two most influential assumptions to produce a low, central and high result.
Key point:
- Transparent assumptions make an approximate answer auditable.
- Never disguise estimates as observed facts or measured statistics.
Source links:
- O*NET OnLine: Data Scientists
42. Q: Why should candidates ask questions of the employer during an interview?
A: Show answer
The chapter says the interview is two-way: the employer assesses the candidate, and the candidate assesses the role, team, culture and opportunities for growth. Asking thoughtful questions demonstrates preparation and genuine interest, while helping the candidate discover whether the environment is suitable. The author recommends having at least two questions ready. Useful topics include the team's current projects, how success is measured, how models are deployed and monitored, collaboration with business users, mentorship, learning support and expectations in the first months.
Study instruction:
- Prepare five questions for a prospective employer and mark which would reveal the most about day-to-day work.
Key point:
- An interview is also the candidate's opportunity to evaluate the employer.
- Questions should reveal how the role works in practice, not merely repeat information on the website.

43. Q: What should a candidate investigate about a company's culture and team before accepting a role?
A: Show answer
A candidate should learn how the team collaborates, who owns data products, how decisions are made, how ethical or privacy concerns are handled, what management expects and whether the company supports learning and career progression. The chapter encourages choosing an employer carefully because the candidate is committing a substantial amount of time to the organization. The candidate can ask about mentorship, code review, model governance, cross-functional work, work-life expectations and how the team responds when results are uncertain. These conversations help distinguish a role that supports development from one that offers only an attractive title.
Study instruction:
- Create a due-diligence checklist for team culture, workload, leadership, learning and ethical practice.
Key point:
- The employer's environment affects both job satisfaction and professional growth.
- Do not evaluate a role only by title or salary; assess the conditions in which the work will be done.

44. Q: How can a data scientist build a reputation after joining a company?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends becoming a helpful resource for data-related questions and showing colleagues how data can improve their work. A new employee can build trust by delivering reliable results, sharing research, explaining findings, supporting other teams and following through on commitments. The goal is to become known for useful contribution rather than self-promotion. Colleagues who understand the value of the work may become advocates for further analytical projects. A reputation grows through repeated, credible delivery and respectful collaboration.
Study instruction:
- Identify one recurring business problem and propose a small analytical improvement you could demonstrate to colleagues.
Key point:
- A professional reputation grows when others experience the value of your work.
- Reliability and knowledge sharing are more sustainable than visibility without delivery.

45. Q: What does the Deloitte management-information dashboard example teach about career growth?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a dashboard project for a retail medical facility that reportedly helped the team save the company $19 million. The author uses the project to show how a visible data contribution can demonstrate value to colleagues and open a conversation about further improvements. The stated savings are a historical case-study figure and should not be generalized to other organizations. The career lesson is that sharing results and inviting suggestions can help make data science part of organizational practice, while giving the analyst a chance to learn from others.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the case as a contribution, a measured or reported result, and a follow-up opportunity; identify what evidence would be needed to validate the savings.
Key point:
- A concrete project outcome can help colleagues see how analytics contributes to operations.
- A reported case result is not a forecast for another project; attribution and baseline matter.

46. Q: How can a data scientist create an analytics culture within an organization?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that practitioners can help create an analytics culture by sharing results, explaining how data can support other functions, inviting feedback and identifying new opportunities. A useful practice is to meet with colleagues involved in a project, show what was learned and discuss what might be improved next time. This encourages broader participation and reduces the perception that data science is an isolated technical service. An analytics culture also needs leadership support, good data access, clear responsibilities and ethical governance; individual advocacy is important but not sufficient on its own.
Study instruction:
- Plan a short project review that shares findings with contributing teams and invites them to propose the next analytical question.
Key point:
- Knowledge-sharing can make analytical work more visible and reusable.
- An analytics culture depends on organizational support as well as individual initiative.

47. Q: Why should employees proactively identify new data opportunities after completing a project?
A: Show answer
The chapter warns that an organization may not know what else to ask a data scientist to do after the original assignment is complete. If the practitioner identifies useful questions, proposes feasible projects and discusses them with a manager, they can help maintain momentum and demonstrate the value of the role. Proactivity should be grounded in business needs and available evidence, not in building models for their own sake. The employee should consider capacity, data access, expected benefit, ownership and the cost of implementation before recommending a new project.
Study instruction:
- Identify one operational problem that may benefit from analysis and prepare a short proposal covering value, cost, data and next step.
Key point:
- Proactive problem-finding can create a path for continued contribution.
- A new project should solve a real problem and have a clear owner and expected benefit.

48. Q: What should a data scientist do if the company is unresponsive to data-driven ideas?
A: Show answer
The chapter suggests that an employee should consider whether the organization is a suitable environment for long-term growth if it repeatedly ignores data-science opportunities. It does not recommend resigning impulsively. Instead, the practitioner should understand the reasons for resistance, communicate value in terms relevant to the business, seek support from managers or colleagues and assess whether there are realistic ways to progress. If the organization persistently offers no meaningful opportunity to grow, the employee may explore other roles while maintaining professional responsibilities and planning a responsible transition.
Study instruction:
- Create a decision tree for responding to organizational resistance, from clarifying concerns through to considering a move.
Key point:
- Not every workplace is equally ready to use data science.
- Assess the environment carefully before deciding whether to stay or seek another role.

49. Q: How should a data-science professional keep a career resilient as tools and roles change?
A: Show answer
A resilient career is built on foundations that can transfer across tools: statistical reasoning, problem formulation, data quality, programming principles, critical evaluation, domain understanding, communication, collaboration and ethical judgement. The chapter emphasizes continual learning, sharing, mentorship and applying strengths to new opportunities. Current occupational sources show that data scientists are expected to analyse data, create and validate models, visualize findings and communicate recommendations; specific software demand changes over time. A professional should therefore track current vacancies and skills data in their target market rather than rely on an old list of fashionable tools or job-growth estimates.
Study instruction:
- Compare current skills requirements in three target vacancies and separate durable capabilities from tool-specific requirements.
Key point:
- Foundational problem-solving and communication skills support adaptation across tools.
- Revisit career plans as employer needs and technology evolve.

50. Q: What are the most important lessons to retain from Chapter 10?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents a broad approach to building a data-science career: understand the different roles, remain flexible about entry routes, choose a field that combines demand with interest, continue learning and apply knowledge to real problems. Build a visible record through projects, open sharing, competitions or volunteering; seek mentors and collaborators; and network by contributing to the community. Tailor applications to employers' needs, prepare for both technical and business interviews, reason transparently through estimation questions and use interviews to assess the company as well. After joining, share results, create advocates, find new problems and evaluate whether the organization supports continued growth. The chapter's older market statistics and examples should be treated as historical context, not current labour-market guidance.
Study instruction:
- Create a 90-day career plan with one learning goal, one portfolio project, one community contribution, one networking action and one employer-research task.
Key point:
- Career growth requires continuing learning, evidence of application and professional relationships.
- Use current market information and evaluate opportunities against both career goals and personal fit.
